Safety Enforcement

12 Safety Enforcement

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why does an admitted plan still need permission on every control tick?

A trajectory can be feasible when it arrives and become unsafe while the machine follows it. The body lags its reference, a payload or a hot motor erodes braking authority, a person steps into the path, or the learned proposer stalls with its last command still in force. A machine that checks only before motion begins discovers these changes after the energy is already moving, and the proposer cannot be trusted to notice them, because nothing in its output bounds stopping distance, actuator effort, or timing. Learned proposals therefore need an independent permission path that re-checks current conditions on every tick, keeps useful motion inside a stopping envelope, corrects a command when a feasible correction exists, and holds a response it can execute without the proposer before the remaining margin is spent. Each of those guarantees depends on bounded sensing error, available actuator force, and a decision delivered on time. Enforcement joins these conditions at the causal boundary, where a permitted command becomes motion that software cannot undo.

Learning Objectives
  • Calculate the finished stopping budget and its speed ceiling, treating the tracking bound as a premise the enforcer monitors on every tick
  • Derive how momentum and commanded effort change the remaining safety margin, and the contact force at a latch and at a handover that caps approach speed
  • Explain how a control barrier function limits motion while its model, sensing, actuator, and timing assumptions hold
  • Formulate a quadratic program that minimally adjusts a proposal within feasible actuator and safety constraints
  • Select state-matched fallback responses when sensing, computation, or actuator limits invalidate normal enforcement
  • Distinguish the permission path’s unrated performance checks from the certified drive, scanner, and arm safety functions that carry the protective claim beneath it
  • Construct an enforcement record and formulate the admitted command that binds each check to its detector, its fallback response, and the records it consumes
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

12.1 The Per-Tick Gatekeeper

Every physical AI system ends in the same act. A command leaves software, becomes current in a motor winding, and puts momentum into a world that does not roll back. Safety enforcement is the runtime mechanism that decides, one control tick at a time, whether the next command may cross that causal boundary. Checking a plan once before it runs does not settle the question, because admission judges the plan against the state the planner predicted, and the machine then executes it against the state it actually reaches. Between those two moments the body can lag its reference, an actuator can run short of torque, a person can step into the aisle, and the learned proposer can stall or emit a setpoint the plan never contained. Enforcement exists so that none of these events is discovered only after the energy has been delivered.

The central constraint is that the learned proposer can neither be trusted with that decision nor be discarded. Its competence is what makes the machine useful, yet nothing in its output bounds stopping distance, actuator effort, or timing. Enforcement resolves the tension by separating proposal from permission, the third law (principle 3), and by giving the permission side three jobs. It must hold a stopping reservation, the clearance the machine needs to arrest its motion from the current state; it must correct a proposal minimally when a feasible correction exists; and it must select a response it can execute without the proposer when none does. The chapter builds the three jobs in that order.

On the warehouse mobile manipulator, the safety microcontroller (MCU) admits the run down the aisle only after the matched stop suffix named in its trajectory record is resident in the MCU’s memory and feasible from the commitment entry state defined in Replacement deadlines and commitment cutoffs. Commitment is the instant after which the machine can no longer abandon the plan’s motion without that latched suffix, so the suffix is matched to the state the machine will have at that instant, not to the states it passes through on the way. Before commitment, a different response must cover the motion. Admission requires an independently executable, state-matched fallback from every state reachable up to that instant. It also requires a bounded representation of the S-curve suffix that the MCU can decode, validate, and execute within its declared workload (the polynomial layout of Trajectory payload is an illustrative encoding rather than that S-curve representation). Because that admission step checks the plan only once before motion starts, the machine still executes the plan one millisecond tick at a time, and enforcement is the check made on every one of those ticks together with the response it selects when even the resident stop no longer fits.

↩︎ The trajectory record and the matched stop suffix it names are established in The Trajectory Record.

The enforcer, the permission path’s per-tick gatekeeper, sits on the permission side of the proposal boundary. It is a hard real-time component that evaluates candidate proposals, verifies invariant state bounds, and holds the exclusive hardware authority to permit current flow or command a fallback stop, keeping the task competence of learned policies while denying them authority over geometric clearance, thermal limits, and human-safety invariants.

↩︎ The per-tick permission cadence on which the enforcer runs is established in Physical time constants and sampling phase lag.

Enforcement sits on the Plan-to-Act edge: no proposal reaches a drive unchecked.

In the architecture this chapter follows, the enforcer executes on a dedicated microcontroller wired to the sensing and drive interfaces it needs. It checks each candidate command before the drive can apply it. A packet firewall offers a limited software analogy: a sender may request an operation without having authority to perform it. Unlike a packet filter, the enforcer must also account for momentum already present in the plant and preserve an achievable response when it refuses a request.

↪ The silicon placement that keeps the permission path independent of the proposer is developed in Silicon Isolation Decisions.

1 Simplex Architecture: The design came from Carnegie Mellon in the 1990s as a way to upgrade the controller of a running plant without stopping it. An untested new controller drove the plant while a simple verified controller and a switching rule stood ready to take it back (Seto et al. 1998). The learned proposer occupies the untested controller’s seat, and the design’s premise, that the region from which the verified controller can still recover is known in advance, is the stopping envelope this chapter computes.

Seto, Danbing, Bruce Krogh, Lui Sha, and Alongkrit Chutinan. 1998. “The Simplex Architecture for Safe Online Control System Upgrades.” Proceedings of the 1998 American Control Conference (ACC) 6: 3504–8. https://doi.org/10.1109/ACC.1998.703255.

The architecture separates a capable proposer, a resident protective controller, and an output selector. This is the Simplex architecture1 (Seto et al. 1998; Sha 2001), the runtime-assurance pattern that The Machine in Five Levels first named. The proposer supplies useful candidates, while the permission path retains the exclusive ability to select a physically feasible response without waiting for it.

The rest of the chapter follows the running machine through that per-tick check. The starting point is the deficit planning left open, when the jerk-limited stop carried the base’s stopping budget past the rack-end clearance at the drive limit while the body’s departure from its reference remained unpriced: the first quantity the enforcer needs is how far the body can stray from the reference it is asked to follow, because every clearance the permission path defends must also contain that error. Once the tracking bound supplies that missing spatial term, the finished stopping budget fixes the reservation and a candidate aisle speed that still requires physical qualification. Actuator saturation then reveals how quickly that reservation shrinks once gravity or thermal limits spend braking authority. A control barrier function turns the reservation into a constraint on the next command. A least-change filter can then correct a violating proposal, both in free space and in contact, inside a declared cycle budget on a processor the proposer cannot preempt. When a check fails or a lease expires, the fallback hierarchy supplies the surviving hardware response, and the chapter closes with the infrastructure failures that break these premises and the enforcement record that binds every check to its evidence.

Even when communication latency is zero and the permission path admits every reference command without delay, physical robots follow reference trajectories imperfectly. Mechanical compliance, inertia, and non-ideal motor dynamics produce tracking discrepancies. The feedback tracker, the closed-loop controller that turns admitted references into actuator effort, must bound this deviation across the entire maneuver. The bound is a worst-case tracking error tube (\(\TrackingErrorBound\)), an upper bound on the spatial departure of the tracked position output from its nominal reference. For Cartesian point tracking, \(\CartesianPosition(\TimeInstant)\) and \(\ReferencePosition(\TimeInstant)\) denote the actual and reference positions evaluated in the same spatial frame. For whole-body systems such as mobile manipulators, the execution stack defines a declared output map or metric weighting that bounds the maximum physical swept-volume displacement in meters. The safety enforcer depends directly on this bound. Any spatial clearance reserved around a planned reference path must guarantee physical containment of the actual robot body.

↩︎ The admitted setpoints and feedforward torques that the tracker turns into actuator effort are established in The Planning Handoff and the Trajectory Record.

Provided that the physical system begins within an initial qualified error region and its operating parameters (reference velocity, command acceleration, payload mass, external disturbances, and actuator temperature) stay within their validated operating envelope, the feedback controller guarantees the transient position-error bound: \[\|\CartesianPosition(\TimeInstant) - \ReferencePosition(\TimeInstant)\| \le \TrackingErrorBound \quad \forall \TimeInstant \ge 0.\] This guarantee forms a software contract between the tracking layer and the safety enforcer. Rather than re-analyzing raw plant dynamics or reconstructing low-level controller state on every millisecond control cycle, the enforcer consumes this certified error bound as an invariant. However, this contract confines physical motion to the \(\TrackingErrorBound\) tube only while the operational conditions used to establish the certificate remain valid.

The magnitude of \(\TrackingErrorBound\) is a physical distance dictated by hardware properties: reflected rotor inertia (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)), gearbox backlash, structural compliance, and actuator torque saturation. While a steady-state stiffness calculation balances constant external disturbances against closed-loop restoring force, steady-state metrics fail to capture transient overshoot, phase lag, or settling oscillations excited during aggressive maneuvers. Consequently, engineering teams cannot rely on static linear approximations; they must support the tracking tube bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) with a bounded plant and controller model, defined operating limits, and full-envelope dynamic qualification tests. Tests can expose an invalid bound; the tested trajectories alone do not prove containment for every admissible maneuver.

↩︎ Reflected rotor inertia, \(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\), is established in Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching.

For the baseline warehouse mobile manipulator, the illustrative dynamic qualification target is \(\TrackingErrorBound =\) 40 mm (illustrative; see the Reader Guide). The tracking controller sustains this bound only while three coupled operational constraints remain satisfied. First, reference trajectories must not demand accelerations exceeding drive torque limits. Second, external disturbance forces must remain below \(\DisturbanceForceBound\), the qualified bound on external disturbance force. Last, actuator coils must operate below their continuous thermal derating thresholds and DC bus voltage limits. A reference trajectory demanding acceleration beyond peak torque drives the actuators into saturation, causing the physical chassis to lag behind the command. Without aggressive anti-windup clamping, the integrator state accumulates severe windup, prolonging recovery long after the reference demand subsides. Outside this qualified operating envelope, the tracking certificate is void, and the system loses its guaranteed error bound.

↩︎ Continuous thermal derating of motor windings is established in Thermal Duty Cycles.

Because the physical robot can deviate from its planned reference by up to \(\TrackingErrorBound\) in any direction, the safety architecture must subtract this uncertainty margin directly from the admissible workspace. Let \(\BarrierFunction\) measure the spatial clearance remaining around a reference. To guarantee that the physical robot maintains non-negative clearance, the enforcer applies an inset contraction: \[\ContractedBarrierFunction(\ReferencePosition) = \BarrierFunction(\ReferencePosition) - \TrackingErrorBound \ge 0.\] A reference trajectory that maintains nominal clearance \(\NominalReferenceClearance\) from a warehouse rack upright guarantees the physical chassis only \(\NominalReferenceClearance - \TrackingErrorBound\) of actual clearance. Consequently, the safety enforcer evaluates reference commands against a contracted boundary that adds 40 mm to the nominal buffer. As detailed in section 12.2, this tracking inset compounds directly with stopping distance budgets, where localization uncertainty and transport delay consume additional clearance. Mechanical wear poses an insidious operational risk here. If worn drive bearings, tire degradation, or altered motor parameters double the true tracking error to 80 mm without updating the enforcer’s parameter registry, the physical base consumes an unbudgeted 40 mm of clearance that the enforcer assumes remains available. An unmodeled tracking degradation silently undermines every higher-level safety margin.

↩︎ Clearance insets that absorb spatial sensing error are established in Spatial Error and Clearance Bounds.

To maintain safety guarantees in production, the runtime monitoring stack must treat \(\TrackingErrorBound\) as an actively verified contract rather than a static design assumption. The underlying real-time middleware publishes \(\TrackingErrorBound\) alongside its validity envelope, while hardware monitors estimate the instantaneous tracking error \(\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant) = \|\CartesianPosition(\TimeInstant) - \ReferencePosition(\TimeInstant)\|\) on every 1 ms controller tick. Agreement with encoder-reported position is a runtime proxy, not independent evidence of physical localization; the localization allowance must contain drift that these channels cannot expose. If an observable disturbance or actuator thermal throttling causes the monitored \(\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant)\) to exceed \(\TrackingErrorBound\), the tracking contract is broken. Upon detecting this violation, the safety enforcer revokes the admitted command and activates a state-matched emergency fallback maneuver. Maintaining physical containment during this transition requires that the fallback braking trajectory remains dynamically feasible even after the tracking bound is lost.

Systems Perspective 12.1: The inset margin invariant
A spatial stopping boundary must reserve margin for the tracking loop’s qualified error bound. The runtime stopping envelope (equation 1) incorporates this geometric inset alongside displacement accumulated during transport lag before admitting reference trajectories. When the safety barrier certificates of section 12.4 are formulated in non-spatial coordinates (such as energy or momentum limits), the safety layer must map the tracking tube into a corresponding conservative bound on barrier variation, rather than subtracting a scalar distance. Evaluating barrier certificates against nominal trajectory geometry alone risks admitting references whose physical tracking tubes intersect obstacle boundaries.

Control theory provides rich methodologies for synthesizing tracking controllers, state observers, and disturbance rejection algorithms (Slotine and Li 1991; Åström and Murray 2021). In embedded industrial robotics, systems frequently deploy a cascaded control hierarchy, where outer position loops feed intermediate velocity setpoints down to high-rate current loops. Alternatively, a tracking Model Predictive Controller (MPC) solves an online optimization problem over a receding horizon to generate optimal corrective control actions while respecting actuator bounds. From the perspective of the safety enforcer, the specific internal architecture of the tracking controller is abstracted away; the safety filter requires only that the chosen implementation certifies and guarantees a concrete tracking bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) under specified operational conditions.

Slotine, Jean-Jacques E, and Weiping Li. 1991. Applied Nonlinear Control. Prentice Hall.

2 Control Lyapunov Function: A Lyapunov function certifies that an unforced system settles; a control Lyapunov function asks only that, at each state, some admissible input make it decrease. Artstein (1983) tied the existence of such a function to stabilizability by feedback, and Sontag (1989) gave an explicit formula that builds the stabilizing input from it. The control barrier function, defined later in this chapter, is its mirror image, keeping the state out of a region instead of pulling it toward a point, and when the two conflict the combined optimization relaxes convergence so that the barrier holds.

Artstein, Zvi. 1983. “Stabilization with Relaxed Controls.” Nonlinear Analysis: Theory, Methods & Applications 7 (11): 1163–73. https://doi.org/10.1016/0362-546X(83)90049-4.
Sontag, Eduardo D. 1989. “A ‘Universal’ Construction of Artstein’s Theorem on Nonlinear Stabilization.” Systems & Control Letters 13 (2): 117–23. https://doi.org/10.1016/0167-6911(89)90028-5.

A tracking controller may establish convergence to the reference path under its qualified model and operating conditions. That result supplies no collision-avoidance guarantee. A well-tuned tracker will follow an erroneous trajectory directly into a warehouse support column with high precision. Control Lyapunov functions2 mathematically formalize this asymptotic convergence toward reference targets; Control Lyapunov functions and stabilization details their theoretical foundation and how optimization pipelines unify stabilizing Lyapunov objectives with the safety barrier certificates this chapter develops in section 12.4.

This division of responsibilities cleanly separates trajectory tracking from safety enforcement. The low-level tracking controller focuses on driving the physical plant toward reference states, while the safety enforcer determines whether those references can be executed without violating invariant boundaries. The tracking error bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) bridges these subsystems by quantifying the spatial divergence between admitted commands and physical execution. Because environmental disturbances, unmodeled payload shifts, or thermal current derating can rapidly widen this error tube, safety architectures must continuously verify physical performance against contracted assumptions. The downstream stopping envelope accounts for this tracking tube alongside sensor latency, braking distance, and localization drift, ensuring that the physical robot remains safely buffered from surrounding hazards.

12.2 Stopping Envelopes

If the warehouse mobile manipulator approaches a rack end at its 1.5 m/s mechanical drive limit when a coworker steps into the aisle, the delay before braking begins and the jerk-limited deceleration curve carry the base past the 1.10 m clearance even before tracking error is counted. That rack-end crossing is the scene in which nearly every chapter since The Physical Body has priced one stopping term: the body supplied braking and its fixed allowances, the Nervous System the lease path, perception the observation age, and planning the jerk-limited stop suffix that exceeded the rack-end clearance at the drive limit (Base stopping budget under jerk-limited deceleration). The tracking bound of The Tracker Handoff and Dynamic Reference Envelopes is the last spatial term. Every one of these terms serves the test that the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), sets, \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). The enforcer applies that test not as one calculation but as a set of states.

Definition 12.1: Stopping envelope

Stopping envelope is the set of plant states, parameterized by clearance and velocity along each degree of freedom, from which the resident emergency braking response, initiated after accounting for all worst-case delays, halts motion before reaching an obstacle boundary while preserving all localization, protective, and tracking margins.

  1. Significance: The envelope translates the abstract irreversibility requirement \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) into an actionable runtime velocity ceiling. When approaching a rack end, the warehouse mobile manipulator’s envelope restricts forward velocity, with the stated dispatch allocation, to at most 1.35 m/s, substantially below its mechanical drive limit of 1.5 m/s.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a static geometric keep-out zone that depends solely on position, the stopping envelope is a dynamic construct governed by speed, system latencies, and braking authority. A given physical clearance safely admits a slow approach while strictly forbidding a high-speed transit.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the stopping envelope as a static, precomputed boundary. In practice, carried payload mass, surface traction coefficients, and motor thermal limits continuously reshape this boundary; calculating safety margins from ideal datasheet decelerations severely overestimates stopping authority when the machine is heavily loaded.

The stopping envelope is the machine’s recoverable set, the states from which the resident stop still succeeds. Inevitable collision states fixes the same boundary in time, as the commitment deadline past which momentum can carry the machine into an inevitable collision state. Velocity enters the recoverable set twice. Delay travel grows linearly with speed and braking travel quadratically, so a clearance that admits a slow approach forbids a fast one at the same position, and the enforcer must keep the machine inside the envelope on every tick, before a fault or an unmodeled disturbance can carry it out. The running calculation uses representative engineering limits; deployment requires each term to be qualified on the actual hardware.

The pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) of Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay already contains the enforcer’s permission cycle, budgeted alongside the lease communication path in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget. One interval stays out of the spatial sum: the dispatch-to-admission delay \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), which The capture-to-brake-onset timing budget of the running machine places between proposal dispatch and admission. The budget charges it instead against the timing allowance that the remaining clearance leaves (section 12.2.1). What the spatial sum still lacks is the tracking error between the commanded reference and the actual plant.

The enforcer accounts for that error with the tracking bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\), offsetting the baseline stopping distance of equation 2.2. It can admit a candidate reference trajectory only if the plant, which may lag that reference by up to \(\TrackingErrorBound\), can still brake to a standstill within the available free distance \(\ClearDistance\). Combining physical clearance, latency, and velocity yields the stopping subtotal before dispatch-to-admission travel is charged: \[\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay + \ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin + \TrackingErrorBound \le \ClearDistance \tag{1}\] Here, \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) represents the physical distance traversed during the braking maneuver from an initial speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\). For idealized constant-deceleration braking, this term simplifies to \(\SpeedAtDetection^2 / (2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\). Under realistic jerk-limited trajectory profiles, \(\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) is governed by equation 11.17, which incorporates the additional displacement accrued during finite jerk transitions. The term \(\LocalizationBound\) denotes the localization uncertainty bound, while \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\) provides an explicit protective safety cushion. The left-hand side sums the baseline stopping distance \(\StoppingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection)\) from equation 2.2 with the tracking inset \(\TrackingErrorBound\).

↪ The full stopping-distance model, tracking inset included, is developed in Kinematic Stopping Envelopes and Information Age Lag.

For complete static-obstacle admission, the delay term uses \(\PrebrakeDelay + \DispatchAdmissionInterval\) in place of \(\PrebrakeDelay\); the resident stop and fixed allowances are unchanged. This inequality defines a conditional stopping subtotal in state space. Within this set, the accounted reaction latency and braking profile fit within the measured obstacle clearance; complete admission must also charge dispatch-to-admission travel. Operating outside this boundary violates the stopping reservation under the qualified model assumptions, though conservative engineering margins do not imply that an instantaneous collision is certain under all conceivable emergency maneuvers. Per-tick enforcement must maintain this stopping reservation over the entire control epoch until the next validated command can take effect, rather than merely verifying instantaneous feasibility at the sample instant.

Whenever a proposed trajectory requires a stopping distance in equation 1 that exceeds the measured clearance along the current heading, the enforcer rejects the command. Because the robot moves directionally, the stopping envelope is anisotropic. Heading dictates which obstacles threaten forward travel and consume braking clearance, while localization uncertainty and the robot’s swept geometric volume demand clearance margins around the entire perimeter.

12.2.1 Sizing the composite stopping budget

Omitting any single interval during which the base keeps moving before or during a stop, or evaluating those intervals under mismatched operating conditions, admits transit speeds that overrun the physical boundary when all delays coincide. The composite stopping budget therefore reserves clearance for every interval in the arrest sequence: sensor transduction lag, compute scheduling delays, actuator engagement, the deceleration profile, and tracking divergence, each evaluated under the same worst-case operating conditions.

For the warehouse mobile manipulator, the tracking bound of 40 mm is the final spatial term. At the 1.5 m/s drive limit, the matched stop suffix had already pushed the cumulative requirement to 1,123.84 mm, past the 1.10 m rack-end clearance; that is the deficit Base stopping budget under jerk-limited deceleration left open. The tracking inset carries the total to 1,163.84 mm, an overshoot of 63.84 mm beyond the physical boundary.

The \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stop suffix replaces idealized constant deceleration (\(\SpeedAtDetection^2 / 2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\)) with the jerk-limited S-curve of The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking, governed by the peak jerk limit \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³. Planning stipulated that bound from the reference \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) polynomial rather than measuring it; the S-curve is the stop the MCU actually runs, and the polynomial supplies only its jerk bound. Because both operating velocities reach the peak deceleration threshold, equation 11.17 models this maneuver by adding the displacement of half a jerk ramp: \[\ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) = \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration} + \SpeedAtDetection\,\frac{\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{2 \JerkLimit}.\] Because this smoothly shaped stop consumes more clearance than discontinuous full-torque braking, the admissible transit speed must be read from the finished budget rather than from the constant-deceleration estimate.

Velocity remains the single operational variable the control architecture can dynamically modulate. Above the characteristic velocity threshold \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit =\) 0.56 m/s, the finite jerk ramps function equivalently to an added delay of \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration/(2\JerkLimit)\) prior to braking at constant deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\). Under these conditions, the pre-dispatch speed ceiling keeps the form of equation 2.3, the positive root of the stopping inequality at equality. The jerk ramps lengthen the pre-brake delay to an effective delay, and the tracking bound comes off the available braking distance: \[\SpeedBound = -\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\EffectiveBrakingDelay + \sqrt{(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\EffectiveBrakingDelay)^2 + 2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\InsetAvailableBrakingDistance}, \qquad \EffectiveBrakingDelay = \PrebrakeDelay + \frac{\CredibleBrakingDeceleration}{2\JerkLimit}, \qquad \InsetAvailableBrakingDistance = \ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin - \TrackingErrorBound \tag{2}\] Below \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\), the deceleration trajectory never reaches the peak limit \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\), rendering stopping distance non-quadratic in velocity; in this low-speed regime, MLSysIM evaluates equation 1 via numerical root-finding. Notebook 12.1 computes this ceiling for the mobile manipulator approaching the aisle rack end.

Napkin Math 12.1: The ceiling the budget sets
Problem: The warehouse mobile manipulator approaches the rack end with rows 1 to 7 of table 1 accounted, before dispatch-to-admission travel. What is the highest speed at which the jerk-limited stop still fits the clear distance, and how much clearance does a candidate aisle speed of 1.3 m/s leave?

Variables:

  • \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (Illustrative; clear distance at the rack end).
  • \(\LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin =\) 50 mm \(+\) 100 mm (Illustrative localization bound; chosen protective clearance), and \(\TrackingErrorBound =\) 40 mm (Illustrative; the qualification target of The Tracker Handoff and Dynamic Reference Envelopes).
  • \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (Illustrative; credible loaded deceleration on a dry floor) and \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived; the peak jerk of the reference \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) polynomial).
  • \(\PrebrakeDelay =\) 133.6 ms (Derived; observation age plus lease path plus brake onset, excluding \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\)).
  • Drive limit 1.5 m/s (Illustrative).

Math: The fixed allowances leave \(\InsetAvailableBrakingDistance =\) 1,100 mm \(-\) 190 mm \(=\) 910 mm. The jerk ramps lengthen the delay to \(\EffectiveBrakingDelay =\) 133.6 ms \(+\) 140.6 ms \(=\) 274.2 ms, so \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\EffectiveBrakingDelay =\) 0.5485 m/s. Under the square root of equation 2, \((\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\EffectiveBrakingDelay)^2 =\) 0.3008 and \(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration{}\InsetAvailableBrakingDistance =\) 3.64 (both in m²/s²), whose sum has root 1.9851 m/s; subtracting 0.5485 m/s gives the ceiling. At 1.3 m/s the terms of table 1 sum to 969.0 mm, and the remainder divided by the speed is the timing allowance.

Result: The pre-dispatch ceiling is \(\SpeedBound =\) 1.44 m/s, below the 1.5 m/s drive limit. The candidate aisle speed leaves 131.0 mm of spare, equivalent to 100.8 ms of unallocated delay.

Systems insight: The irreversibility requirement \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\), rather than motor electrical capability, dictates the operating speed. Running at the nominal drive limit violates the verified spatial budget, and the speed ceiling shifts strictly through the parameters governing the quadratic root and effective latency \(\EffectiveBrakingDelay\): available clearance, qualified braking deceleration, fixed margins, and end-to-end transport lag. Any latency introduced by downstream processing directly consumes the 100.8 ms timing allowance, which cannot be arbitrarily replenished.

The candidate transit speed, 1.3 m/s, sits below that ceiling, and its 131.0 mm margin is the only clearance left to absorb unmodeled pipeline delays or to serve as a reserve. That margin is narrow relative to the failure modes it must absorb. If chunk lease enforcement were entrusted to a non-real-time host operating system, an upstream planning stall lasting 200 ms instead of the nominal 60 ms lease would inject 182 mm of unbraked displacement, causing the base to overrun the rack clearance by 50.99 mm. This hazard shows why lease termination must be autonomously enforced by dedicated microcontroller hardware directly gating motor power (section 12.5).

The observation-age term ends at dispatch, and the chunk lease begins at admission, so the interval between them, \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\), belongs to neither. The complete operational delay therefore expands to \(\AdmissionInclusivePrebrakeDelay = \PrebrakeDelay + \DispatchAdmissionInterval\), requiring an additional spatial reservation of \(\SpeedAtDetection\DispatchAdmissionInterval\). This phase encompasses any remaining neural inference, safety envelope projection, and inter-process communication; pipelined or overlapping stages must be budgeted carefully to avoid double-counting. A newly arrived sensor observation updates this budget only when the hardware pipeline delivers and validates it prior to actuation. Laid out on one time axis (figure 2), the observation age ends at dispatch, the dispatch-to-admission interval follows, and the lease, one tick, one bus cycle, and drive onset bring the base to brake onset. A travel row beneath converts each interval into millimeters at the candidate aisle speed.

At this candidate speed, dividing the 131.0 mm spare distance by the speed yields 100.8 ms of unallocated timing allowance. Added to the 51.6 ms observation age, that allowance places the latest admission after capture at 152.4 ms. Added to the 133.6 ms pre-brake delay, it places the latest brake onset after capture at 234.4 ms. The dispatch-to-admission interval spends part of that allowance and leaves whatever remains as the reserve; because operating right at these boundaries spends all available clearance, the figures are qualification constraints rather than performance claims. The budget carries forward the allocation that the placement chapter makes for this interval (The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval), \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 63.3 ms for the partitioned compute pipeline. That allocation consumes 82.3 mm of the spatial margin, leaves a cushion of 37.5 ms (48.7 mm of distance), and puts total latency from capture to brake onset at 196.9 ms. Because that allocation is an engineering budget rather than a measurement, an instrumented campaign with hardware timestamps must confirm it before the vehicle can be certified at this speed. Read row by row (table 1, figure 1), the terms through observation age fit inside the clear distance at the 1.5 m/s drive limit, whereas the jerk-limited stop (row 6) carries the total past the line and the tracking inset (row 7) widens the breach. At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, those same terms stop 131.0 mm short of the line and still leave a 48.7 mm reserve once dispatch-to-admission travel is charged.

This baseline stopping model evaluates a stationary obstacle situated at distance \(\ClearDistance\), such as a dropped warehouse tote or a pedestrian who has stepped out from an aisle rack and stopped. Under this assumption, the hazard remains fixed from the instant braking begins. If, instead, a pedestrian continues walking toward the vehicle throughout the reaction and braking phases, the obstacle actively closes the safety gap from the opposing direction. Before dispatch allocation, an ongoing pedestrian approach restricts the mobile base to a subtotal ceiling of just 0.26 m/s at this rack end.

This drastic velocity reduction occurs because an active hazard penalizes the stopping budget by the duration of the stop rather than merely its displacement. Assuming an ISO 13855 approach velocity \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound =\) 1.6 m/s across the entire reaction and stopping interval \(\PrebrakeDelay + \StoppingDuration(\SpeedAtDetection)\), the spatial budget acquires an additional closure penalty \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound\,[\PrebrakeDelay + \StoppingDuration(\SpeedAtDetection)]\) absent from static calculations. When operating below the velocity threshold \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit =\) 0.56 m/s, the jerk-limited braking profile cannot achieve peak deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\); it ramps deceleration up and immediately down in a triangular profile lasting \(2\sqrt{\SpeedAtDetection/\JerkLimit}\). This duration diminishes far more slowly than the linear scaling \(\SpeedAtDetection/\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) as velocity decreases. Initiating a jerk-limited stop from 0.26 m/s requires 382 ms, compared to only 130 ms for idealized step deceleration. While the mobile base travels only 50 mm during braking, the pedestrian advances 612 mm toward the robot over the same window. Achieving peak deceleration from such low speeds would demand a jerk of 41 m/s³, several times the stipulated bound the load was assumed to tolerate, whereas unconstrained step deceleration would permit 0.64 m/s. That contrast arises because the jerk bound costs differently in the two regimes. Above \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\) it adds a fixed delay of \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration/(2\JerkLimit)\) whose travel grows with speed and forms the row-6 term pushing the drive limit past the clear distance. Below that threshold it stretches the stop’s duration. Against a person who keeps walking, duration is what the budget pays for, so reducing operating speed yields diminishing safety returns.

↩︎ The pedestrian intrusion distance, evaluated for a constant-deceleration stop at the drive limit, is established in Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay.

Throttling nominal aisle velocity to the walking-person ceiling would severely degrade warehouse transport throughput for an event that facility layout and procedural crossing rules can prevent. The baseline operational design therefore mandates enforced physical or optical crossing restrictions at aisle ends, while the residual-claims register in A residual-claims register tracks the governing premise that pedestrian incursions into the stopping zone are procedurally prevented. Operating at the candidate aisle velocity remains contingent on empirical validation of these access restrictions. The derivation bounds stopping against a static obstruction; protecting against an unconstrained approaching pedestrian requires certified physical interlocks or external safety scanners.

Three operating regimes therefore set three ceilings (figure 3). On a dry floor, fixed allowances, linear delay travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay\), quadratic braking travel \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration)\), and jerk-ramp travel sum to a total that meets the 1.10 m clear distance at 1.44 m/s. Even an optimistic all-weight traction screen requires a floor friction coefficient of at least 0.204, because \(\TractionLimitedDeceleration = \min(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration, \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity)\) assumes all weight contributes braking traction. The running machine also requires qualified driven-wheel load share, torque, and stability (Kinetic Momentum). On a sealed floor inspected to \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient =\) 0.12, the ceiling falls to 1.23 m/s. A pedestrian walking toward the base at 1.6 m/s charges the stop by its duration and contracts the ceiling to 0.26 m/s. Because these three roots are subtotal ceilings before dispatch allocation, charging the 63.3 ms allocation lowers each of them. The dry-floor root falls to 1.35 m/s by adding the base’s travel through dispatch. The optimistic inspected-floor root falls to 1.17 m/s in the same way. The walking-person root falls to 0.19 m/s by adding both the base’s travel and the person’s closure across that interval. Because operating at any maximum root spends all clearance and leaves zero reserve, the remaining reserve must be recomputed for the selected speed, floor, and hazard motion. The 37.5 ms timing reserve belongs specifically to the dry, static-obstacle 1.3 m/s candidate, and an authority transition cannot claim its original 100.8 ms margin a second time.

↪ The authority transitions that must fit inside this remaining reserve are developed in Authority Transitions.

An independent protective safety scanner operates under its own hardware-to-actuation timing budget. When an obstacle breaches a safety-rated zone, the scanner’s internal optical sampling, fail-safe output switching, safety controller logic, and drive disconnect delays entirely bypass the perception dispatch and software lease infrastructure. However, the physical response must still reserve the same underlying deceleration distance, mechanical tracking error, localization tolerance, and protective safety cushion; only computational delays bypassed by the safety bus are eliminated from the sum. Industrial mobile bases deploy such protective fields under ISO 3691-4 (International Organization for Standardization 2023b) for driverless industrial trucks and ISO 13849-1 (International Organization for Standardization 2023a) for safety-related control circuits, using a safety-rated planar scanner of the kind in figure 4, which evaluates programmable warning and protective fields. The physical dimensions of this protective field must be calculated from the dedicated scanner path’s verified hardware timing budget, rather than inheriting the software lease and vision latencies of the neural planner. The safety-rated optical scanner is a component of a separately qualified protective function, distinct from the primary perception LiDAR that generates non-safety-rated point clouds for neural proposal generation. Its component rating does not certify the complete sensor-to-stop path. In the running architecture, that path must establish the detection coverage, response time, and physical stopping performance required for protection at rack intersections. Section 12.8.3 maps the software stopping budget onto this certified hardware baseline and shows that industrial vehicle testing standards validate pedestrian detection against the same static-obstacle assumption derived here.

12.2.2 Phase portraits and speed ceilings

Checking velocity against a fixed scalar limit fails as soon as obstacle distance shrinks: a speed that is recoverable two meters from the rack end becomes an inevitable collision eighty centimeters closer. Drawn in the distance–velocity \((\ClearDistance, \SpeedAtDetection)\) phase plane (figure 5), the plotted pre-dispatch envelope separates states whose accounted subtotal fits from states whose subtotal already exceeds clearance. Complete admission contracts that boundary further by charging dispatch travel. Braking alone traces the outer parabola. The inset boundary pulls the viable set, the recoverable set drawn in this plane, inward by the pre-brake travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay\), the jerk ramps of the matched stop, and the fixed localization, protective, and tracking allowances. At the rack-end clear distance the height of that plotted boundary is the subtotal speed ceiling, with the candidate aisle speed beneath it. The complete dry-floor ceiling is 1.35 m/s; the governor must use the fully charged boundary. Under the verified delay and deceleration bounds, the envelope is therefore a velocity ceiling that varies with clearance, and a state outside it violates the stopping reservation even if an emergency maneuver might avoid a collision in an edge case.

Evaluated on every enforcer tick, the envelope becomes a speed governor that runs in two stages, and their order matters:

  1. Current-state feasibility check: The enforcer first verifies whether the robot’s current operational state \((\ClearDistance, \SpeedAtDetection)\) remains strictly inside the envelope. It tests whether a full braking response from the current velocity \(\SpeedAtDetection\), with all transport lags, jerk transitions, and margin allowances, fits within the currently measured clearance \(\ClearDistance\). If this condition fails, no newly proposed command setpoint can restore the lost stopping reservation, because reducing the target acceleration cannot retroactively erase the kinetic energy or displacement accrued from the existing physical velocity. A violation at this step immediately selects STOP, the resident controlled stop that section 12.8 defines among the enforcer’s fallback responses.
  2. Candidate command projection: If and only if the current state passes validation, the enforcer samples the speed ceiling \(\SpeedBound(\ClearDistance)\) from the fully charged counterpart of the pre-dispatch boundary shown in figure 5. It then clamps the candidate velocity proposal \(\CandidateSpeedCommand\) to the admissible ceiling, admitting \(\AdmittedSpeedCommand = \min\bigl(\CandidateSpeedCommand, \max(0, \SpeedBound)\bigr)\). Rather than discarding an over-speed proposal entirely, this governor projects the command onto the boundary of the safe set.

If obstacle clearance shrinks, computational jitter expands end-to-end latency, or floor friction degrades, the envelope boundary shifts inward, throttling admissible velocity on the subsequent control cycle when qualified sensing or configuration evidence exposes the change. A reference clamp alone does not establish that actual velocity remains inside the envelope between checks. This one-dimensional velocity governor is the scalar prototype for multi-dimensional safety filters; in section 12.4, control barrier functions extend this exact principle to enforce forward invariance over full state spaces.

↩︎ The clearance-parameterized velocity ceiling is established as a kinematic constraint in Dynamic velocity ceilings and clearance budgeting.

The stopping envelope is the kinematic roofline of Delay consumes stopping clearance carried through the finished budget. Physical clearance (\(\ClearDistance\)) sets a ceiling on transit speed for the qualified braking, margin, and timing bounds. A faster inference frame rate raises that ceiling only if it reduces a delay charged to the stopping budget. Model size and inference optimizations affect the ceiling through their share of the sensor-to-actuator delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\). On the warehouse mobile manipulator, every 1 ms of added pipeline delay consumes 1.3 mm of spatial clearance at nominal aisle velocity, and an unbudgeted 10 ms latency increase depresses the allowable speed ceiling by approximately 14 mm/s. Consequently, deploying an oversized vision-language model that increases latency directly lowers maximum transit speed, whereas an inference improvement that does not change the scheduled delivery tick may leave the charged delay unchanged. The speed ceiling shifts materially only when altering the physical parameters of the plant: verified deceleration authority \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\), end-to-end transport lag \(\PrebrakeDelay\), tracking error bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\), and localization uncertainty \(\LocalizationBound\).

None of these governing parameters remain stationary during real-world operation. Manipulating a heavy payload in the end-effector increases arm inertia, diminishing joint deceleration limits within peak motor torque bounds. Industrial floor contaminants such as oil or water severely degrade tire traction beneath the assumed braking limit \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\), while aggressive operational cycles heat motor windings and drive inverters into thermal derating. A safety enforcer that treats \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) as an immutable datasheet constant will dangerously underestimate stopping distance under heavy workloads. Runtime safety monitors must update braking bounds as observable, qualified payload and thermal states evolve. Instantaneous wheel-floor friction is not measured on this machine; an unobserved floor change invalidates the inspection premise and requires an operating restriction rather than automatic adaptation. In either case, relying on unvalidated, optimistic estimates invalidates the formal safety contract.

Dynamic adaptation remains effective only as long as state estimators remain uncorrupted and physical actuators retain control authority. If severe surface contamination reduces friction toward zero, or if sustained high-torque demands push drive inverters beyond their thermal trip thresholds, physical braking authority collapses. When required stopping torque exceeds what the physical hardware can exert against the environment, software can still detect the boundary violation, but it can no longer prevent an impact.

12.3 Authority Exhaustion and Emergency Fallback

A loaded arm descending toward the packing station must counter gravity before any torque remains to slow its motion. Actuator saturation occurs when requested effort reaches the electrical, mechanical, or thermal limits of the physical body, which can cause downstream integral controllers to accumulate windup.3 The actuator cannot deliver the corrective control action \(\CertifiedControlAction\) at its commanded magnitude in any of several cases. Motor phase currents may hit inverter current-chopping limits (\(\InverterCurrentLimit\)). The DC bus may sag under inductive switching transients (\(\PowerRailInductance\,d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\)). Back-EMF at high rotor speeds may collapse voltage headroom (\(\DcRailVoltage - \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed \to 0\)). Winding temperatures may approach thermal derating thresholds (\(\WindingTemperature \to \WindingTemperatureLimit\)). No mathematical safety boundary can be defended with control effort the physical body cannot generate.

3 Integrator Windup: A proportional-integral controller’s integral term continues summing tracking error while an actuator sits at its saturation limit, as standard feedback loops do not inherently know that command outputs are clipped. When tracking error reverses, this accumulated state must unwind before the actuator command can leave the saturation rail. That unwinding delay directly consumes safety clearance. While modern anti-windup schemes clamp or back-calculate integrator state during saturation (Åström and Murray 2021), the safety enforcer must still budget whatever residual unwinding delay remains in the low-level hardware controller.

Åström, Karl Johan, and Richard M Murray. 2021. Feedback Systems: An Introduction for Scientists and Engineers. Princeton university press.

↩︎ The electrical and transmission limits that bound delivered actuator effort are established in Actuator Transmission Limits.

When required braking effort exceeds physical actuator limits, no safety filter can enforce the promised stopping horizon. An emergency fallback invoked after control authority is lost can mitigate damage, but it cannot restore an invariant guarantee that physical dynamics have already broken. The runtime enforcer must therefore contract its forward reservation before actuator effort saturates, preserving a feasible braking margin under worst-case loading.

A stopping envelope based on peak torque alone overstates braking authority when gravity drives the moving load toward a hazard. Under a steady-state bias torque \(\JointBiasTorque\), the effective deceleration available to arrest angular motion collapses: \[\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration = \frac{\JointTorqueLimit - \JointBiasTorque}{\EffectiveJointInertia} \tag{3}\] where \(\EffectiveJointInertia\) is mechanism inertia and \(\JointTorqueLimit\) is the peak dynamic torque limit. In linear translational systems with mass \(\Mass\) and bias force \(\ActuatorBiasEffort\), equation 3 takes the equivalent form \(\NetBrakingDeceleration = (\MaximumActuatorEffort - \lvert \ActuatorBiasEffort\rvert) / \Mass\). For motion aligned with the bias, a payload consuming 75 percent of peak opposing torque leaves only the remaining quarter for active braking, quadrupling the constant-deceleration stopping distance at that speed.

Gravitational and dynamic biases are directionally asymmetric. When motion opposes gravity (such as during an upward arm swing), gravity assists motor braking, yielding \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration = (\JointTorqueLimit + \JointBiasTorque) / \EffectiveJointInertia\). Conversely, when motion aligns with the bias (such as a loaded arm descending toward a workstation), gravity directly opposes deceleration, collapsing braking headroom to \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration = (\JointTorqueLimit - \JointBiasTorque) / \EffectiveJointInertia\). Because safety certificates must guarantee forward invariance in the worst-case physical direction, the runtime enforcer must evaluate \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration\) with directional sign awareness rather than treating braking authority as an isotropic scalar. Suppose the warehouse mobile manipulator carries a mug from the takeaway conveyor down toward a coworker at the packing station. Its tool center point (TCP) moves at 0.96 m/s, within its 1 m/s free-space limit (only the final approach to the coworker’s hand runs at the reduced handover speed derived in section 12.6.2). Gravitational bias during this descent quadruples the tool’s stopping distance (notebook 12.2).

Napkin Math 12.2: The gravity braking tax
Problem: The loaded joint descends toward the packing station at \(\JointAngularSpeed_0\). How far does the tool point travel before the joint stops when the whole peak torque brakes, and how far when gravity on the arm and mug holds part of it?

Variables:

  • \(\JointAngularSpeed_0 =\) 1.2 rad/s and \(\LeverArmLength =\) 0.80 m (Illustrative; the joint speed and effective link lever arm of the scenario, see the Reader Guide).
  • \(\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 3 kg·m² (Illustrative; joint inertia with the mug).
  • \(\JointTorqueLimit =\) 87 N·m (Datasheet; peak torque of the registered arm’s axes 1 to 4).
  • \(\JointBiasTorque =\) 65.25 N·m, 75 percent of peak torque (Illustrative; gravity on the arm and mug in the direction of motion).

Math: With no bias, \(\UnbiasedAngularBrakingDeceleration = \JointTorqueLimit/\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 29 rad/s², the stopping angle is \(\JointAngularSpeed_0^2/(2\UnbiasedAngularBrakingDeceleration) =\) 0.0248 rad (1.42\(^\circ\)), and the tool point travels \(\LeverArmLength\,\FiniteChange\JointAngle =\) 19.86 mm. On the descent, equation 3 leaves \(\JointTorqueLimit - \JointBiasTorque =\) 21.75 N·m for braking, so \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration =\) 7.25 rad/s², the stopping angle is 0.0993 rad (5.69\(^\circ\)), and the tool travels 79.45 mm.

Result: The descending stop is four times the unbiased one, an unbudgeted overrun of 59.59 mm for an enforcer that assumes the datasheet torque.

Systems insight: Stopping distance scales as \(1/\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration\), so a bias that takes 75 percent of the torque quadruples the braking term of \(\StoppingDistance\) at the same speed. The certificate must use the net authority in the worst direction, not the actuator’s rating.

The asymmetry is direction, not magnitude (figure 6). On the descent, gravity torque acts with the motion and leaves only 21.75 N·m of the motor’s peak for braking, while the same joint moving up recruits gravity and stops in less distance than the unbiased case. Whether the descending overrun reaches the workstation depends on the clearance left when braking begins, the margin the barrier certificate of section 12.4 evaluates. The coworker’s hand must never fall within the arm’s biased stopping distance of 79.45 mm plus its blind travel during sensing and communication delay before the handover channel engages.

An unprotected integrator downstream of the safety enforcer can introduce severe latency during actuator saturation. If clipping does not halt integration, tracking error continues to accumulate in the controller state. When the safety system subsequently requests maximum braking, the command must pass through this wound-up state rather than bypassing it. The actuator command consequently remains pinned at its forward saturation limit while the integrator unwinds. Take an illustrative case in which tracking error accumulates for 200 ms and the unwinding phase then delays net braking deceleration by \(\IntegratorUnwindDelay =\) 150 ms. The two durations are independent assumptions rather than one derived from the other; the unwind time depends on the integrator gain and on how sharply the error reverses, and only a test of the actual drive fixes it. At an aisle speed of 1.3 m/s, this latency adds \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\IntegratorUnwindDelay =\) 195 mm of unbraked travel. That overrun exceeds the system’s entire 131.0 mm unallocated clearance buffer by 63.99 mm, even before dispatch-to-admission latency is charged. Classical techniques such as integrator clamping, back-calculation, and conditional integration prevent windup in the feedback loop (Åström and Hägglund 2006; Franklin et al. 1998). The enforcement architecture, however, must explicitly quantify any residual unwinding delay and budget the corresponding unbraked distance into the stopping horizon.

Åström, Karl Johan, and Tore Hägglund. 2006. Advanced PID Control. ISA-The Instrumentation, Systems,; Automation Society.
Franklin, Gene F, J David Powell, and Michael L Workman. 1998. Digital Control of Dynamic Systems. 3rd ed. Addison-Wesley.

Actuator saturation is one of the few hardware limitations with an immediate, deterministic detector inside the permission path. On each 1 ms tick, the permission path compares the commanded effort \(\CommandedActuatorEffort\) against the realizable actuator envelope: \(\RealizedActuatorEffort = \text{clip}(\CommandedActuatorEffort, \MinimumActuatorEffort, \MaximumActuatorEffort)\). A nonzero residual \(\CommandedActuatorEffort - \RealizedActuatorEffort \neq 0\) or an active drive-stage current-limit flag directly reveals that the requested effort has been clipped. This comparison detects saturation at the command interface, however, rather than measuring delivered shaft torque or tire-ground traction. Qualified physical evidence (phase currents, DC bus voltage, thermal derating thresholds, and kinematic state telemetry) must support a conservative lower bound on available directional braking deceleration. An upper actuator-effort ceiling limits possible braking; it does not establish the braking that a stop may credit, and a clipping detector does not measure tire-floor traction.

Because degraded authority immediately lengthens stopping distance, the enforcer updates its braking envelope in real time. Before admitting the next trajectory segment, the filter recomputes the effective deceleration \(\NetBrakingDeceleration = (\MaximumActuatorEffort - \lvert \ActuatorBiasEffort\rvert) / \Mass\). Allowable vehicle velocity scales down as authority contracts; if the recalculated stopping distance no longer fits within the confirmed clear distance, the enforcer must command immediate deceleration while sufficient braking headroom remains.

Authority exhaustion must also be communicated upstream to the planner. If a 20 \(\text{Hz}\) trajectory generator continues commanding aggressive accelerations while the low-level 1 kHz loop reports saturation, the policy is planning trajectories for a virtual body that the physical robot cannot realize. The permission path treats saturation as an active constraint violation. It revokes the active trajectory lease and forces a replan initialized from the machine’s actual, authority-constrained dynamic state.

Systems Perspective 12.2: The actuator authority invariant
A control barrier function, as formulated in Safety and Control Barrier Functions, remains mathematically valid only while physical actuators retain sufficient current, voltage, and thermal headroom to deliver the computed corrective effort: \(\ActuatorEffort^* \in [\MinimumActuatorEffort, \MaximumActuatorEffort]\). When steady-state gravity, friction, or payload inertia encroaches on this operating envelope, the runtime enforcer must dynamically contract the safe set before marginal control authority vanishes.

Checkpoint 12.1: Stopping envelope and actuator headroom

Consider how the stopping envelope, and the actuator authority beneath it, bound permissible motion:

12.4 Safe Sets as Conditional Permission

Once gravitational bias torque is counted, the descending arm analyzed in section 12.3 requires 79.45 mm of tool travel to brake to a complete stop, and this stopping distance scales quadratically with approach speed. A clearance margin that guarantees safety at one velocity fails completely at a higher velocity. The runtime safety enforcer therefore requires a joint predicate over position and velocity that it can evaluate synchronously on every tick of the control clock.

While a stopping budget establishes a static physical margin, a control barrier function4 transforms that spatial margin into an instantaneous constraint on the next actuator command (Ames et al. 2014; Ames et al. 2019). This formulation belongs to the broader family of safety filters that monitor candidate action proposals against certified fallbacks, intervening only when necessary (Hsu et al. 2024) so that the machine can execute aggressive tasks while guaranteeing sufficient braking authority to halt.

4 Barrier Function: In constrained optimization, an interior barrier term such as \(-\log(-\InequalityConstraintFunction(\OptimizationIterate))\) grows without bound near constraint boundaries, preventing numerical iterates from escaping the feasible set. Barrier certificates extended this mathematical principle to dynamical systems, proving that no trajectory starting in a safe set can ever reach an unsafe state (Prajna and Jadbabaie 2004). Control barrier functions subsequently formulated this certificate as an explicit, affine condition on the instantaneous control input (Wieland and Allgöwer 2007). While an optimization algorithm’s safety guarantee holds because its numerical iterates are evaluated exactly in software, a physical plant’s safety guarantee holds only as long as actuator limits, dynamics, and real-time execution match the model assumed by the certificate.

Prajna, Stephen, and Ali Jadbabaie. 2004. “Safety Verification of Hybrid Systems Using Barrier Certificates.” Hybrid Systems: Computation and Control (HSCC), Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 2993: 477–92. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-24743-2_32.
Wieland, Peter, and Frank Allgöwer. 2007. “Constructive Safety Using Control Barrier Functions.” IFAC Proceedings Volumes 40 (12): 462–67. https://doi.org/10.3182/20070822-3-ZA-2920.00076.

5 Nagumo’s Theorem: Nagumo (1942) established that, under conditions ensuring unique solutions, a closed set is forward-invariant if and only if the system vector field points into the set or remains tangent to its boundary at every boundary point; viability theory subsequently formalized and generalized this principle. Because Nagumo’s condition applies to continuous-time flows, a digital controller executing at periodic control ticks requires an inter-sample buffer, sized among the conditions forward invariance rests on (section 12.6.3), to guarantee physical safety between discrete updates.

Nagumo, Mitio. 1942. “Über Die Lage Der Integralkurven Gewöhnlicher Differentialgleichungen.” Proceedings of the Physico-Mathematical Society of Japan, 3rd Series 24: 551–59. https://doi.org/10.11429/ppmsj1919.24.0_551.

A safe set \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) collects all system states for which a scalar certificate \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) remains nonnegative. Its boundary represents zero margin, whereas its interior provides positive margin. For the manipulator arm, both position and velocity must enter this certificate because a physical clearance that safely accommodates a slow approach must reject a high-speed approach at the identical position: \[\ForwardInvariantSafeSet = \{\MechanicalPlantState \in \PlantStateSpace \mid \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge 0\}, \quad \SafetyBoundaryManifold = \{\MechanicalPlantState \in \PlantStateSpace \mid \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = 0\}, \quad \text{Int}(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet) = \{\MechanicalPlantState \in \PlantStateSpace \mid \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) > 0\}.\] Ensuring that trajectories remain inside \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) is known as forward invariance, the physical counterpart to an inductive loop invariant in formal software verification: \[\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState_0) \ge 0 \quad \land \quad \BarrierFunctionRate(\MechanicalPlantState, \ControlInput) \ge -\ClassKFunction(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)) \quad \implies \quad \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState_t) \ge 0 \quad \forall \TimeInstant \ge 0. \tag{4}\] This implication holds in continuous time under valid plant models and actuator feasibility. Nagumo’s theorem5 (Blanchini 1999) provides the geometric boundary interpretation: the closed-loop vector field at the boundary \(\SafetyBoundaryManifold\) must point tangent to or back into the set. The comparison function \(\ClassKFunction(\cdot)\), chosen as an extended class-\(\Glyph{\mathcal{K}}\) function, strictly increases with safety margin and vanishes at zero. This allows rapid approach motions deep inside the interior of \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) while enforcing a nondecreasing safety margin along the perimeter. In an embedded implementation, discrete-time sampling, state estimation noise, and torque delivery latency demand additional safety buffers before continuous-time invariance translates into physical reliability.

Definition 12.2: Control barrier function

A control barrier function is a continuously differentiable scalar function \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) of the plant state whose 0-superlevel set defines the safe set \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\), and whose directional derivative can be directly influenced by the control input. Restricting actuation to control inputs that satisfy \(\BarrierFunctionRate(\MechanicalPlantState, \ControlInput) \ge -\ClassKFunction(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState))\) guarantees that any trajectory initialized within \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) remains inside \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) for all time (forward invariance, equation 4), provided the plant model, actuator limits, and timing assumptions remain valid.

  1. Significance: The function translates an empirical stopping margin into an admissible half-space constraint on the next control cycle. For the descending arm of section 12.3, its zero level set sits exactly where remaining tool clearance equals the biased stopping distance of 79.45 mm; closer than this boundary, no admissible torque command can keep the system within \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\).
  2. Distinction: Whereas a Lyapunov function certifies asymptotic convergence toward an equilibrium state, a control barrier function certifies set invariance, proving that system trajectories never exit an admissible operating region. The barrier function provides the mathematical certificate; the least-change quadratic program filter of section 12.6 provides the runtime mechanism to enforce it.
  3. Common pitfall: Formulating \(\BarrierFunction\) strictly as raw geometric distance. Because distance has relative degree two with respect to torque (commanded torque produces acceleration, which integrates to velocity, which in turn integrates to position), the control input has zero direct authority over \(\BarrierFunctionRate\) at the boundary. A filter monitoring position alone reacts only after physical momentum has made collision unavoidable. An effective barrier must incorporate approach velocity and net braking deceleration, as shown in section 12.4.1.

12.4.1 Kinetic barrier certificates and relative degree

On the descending arm, a barrier function written purely in terms of remaining spatial distance \(\AngularClearance\) leaves commanded motor torque out of its first time derivative, so the safety filter remains blind to closing velocity until the arm reaches the boundary with momentum it can no longer arrest. Across the causal boundary, high-level software proposals meet continuous physical plant dynamics governed by a control-affine differential equation: \[\PlantStateRate = \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) + \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\ControlInput\] where \(\ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) captures unforced physical drift (including inertia, Coriolis forces, gravitational bias, and passive friction), \(\ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) maps control inputs to generalized accelerations, and \(\ControlInput\) represents delivered joint torques. On this robot, embedded motor drives close the internal current and velocity loops, meaning the MCU does not write raw voltages to the coils; the per-tick cycle described in section 12.6.4 converts a torque-space decision into the setpoint and torque limit the drive accepts under the qualified delivered-effort realization contract developed there.

Evaluating how a scalar barrier certificate \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) evolves along state trajectories requires computing its directional time derivative. By the multivariable chain rule, the total time derivative \(\BarrierFunctionRate\) expands across the state velocity \(\PlantStateRate\): \[\BarrierFunctionRate(\MechanicalPlantState, \ControlInput) = \frac{d\BarrierFunction}{d\TimeInstant} = \frac{\partial \BarrierFunction}{\partial \MechanicalPlantState} \PlantStateRate = \nabla \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \PlantStateRate = \underbrace{\nabla \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)}_{\LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\MechanicalPlantState)} + \underbrace{\left(\nabla \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \cdot \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\right)}_{\LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState)} \ControlInput\] Nonlinear control theory designates \(\LieDerivativeAlongDrift\) and \(\LieDerivativeAlongControl\) as Lie derivatives.6 In modern computational systems, these directional derivatives are standard Jacobian-vector products (JVPs) supported natively by autodiff frameworks such as JAX and PyTorch: \[\LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState) = \text{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)), \qquad \LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState) = \nabla \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState) = \text{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)).\] In forward-mode automatic differentiation, a JVP evaluates the derivative along a single tangent vector. When the control matrix \(\ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) contains multiple input columns \(\ControlAffineInputField_j(\MechanicalPlantState)\), the notation \(\operatorname{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineInputField)\) denotes the row vector of individual channel products \(\operatorname{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineInputField_j)\). The drift term and the control-channel terms capture distinct physical dynamics:

6 Lie Derivative (the derivative along a flow): Named after Sophus Lie, whose continuous transformation groups generate the flows along which the derivative is taken, the Lie derivative in control theory is identical to the Jacobian-vector product in modern scientific computing. Because both describe the same directional derivative, the automatic differentiation frameworks used during neural policy training can directly evaluate drift and control authority for learned or tabulated barrier functions, whereas the embedded safety enforcer executes the analytical closed form.

  1. \(\LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\MechanicalPlantState) = \text{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState))\) is the system drift JVP, measuring the rate at which safety margin changes due solely to momentum, gravitational load, and passive dynamics.
  2. \(\LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState) = \text{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState))\) is the control authority JVP, measuring the instantaneous sensitivity of the barrier rate of change to commanded actuator torque across the causal boundary.

Formulating an effective barrier certificate requires avoiding the relative degree trap. In computational graph terms, relative degree is the graph distance, counted in time-integrator nodes, between the commanded control input \(\ControlInput\) and the barrier certificate \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\). If an engineer defines a safety certificate purely as geometric clearance, \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) = \ObstaclePosition - \RobotPosition \ge 0\), the commanded actuation is separated from position by two successive integrations: \[\text{Actuation } \ActuatorEffort \xrightarrow{\int \frac{1}{\Mass} d\TimeInstant} \text{Velocity } \RobotApproachVelocity \xrightarrow{\int d\TimeInstant} \text{Position } \RobotPosition\] This two-stage integration cascade establishes relative degree \(\BarrierRelativeDegree = 2\). Taking the first time derivative of clearance yields the closing velocity, \(\BarrierFunctionRate = -\RobotApproachVelocity\), which contains no control input whatsoever (\(\LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState) = \text{JVP}(\BarrierFunction, \MechanicalPlantState, \ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)) = \ZeroVector\)). Because the control authority JVP evaluates to zero, motor torque has zero instantaneous authority over the rate of barrier decay. At the boundary of the safe set (\(\BarrierFunction = 0\)), enforcing forward invariance \(\BarrierFunctionRate \ge -\ClassKFunction(0) = 0\) requires \(-\RobotApproachVelocity \ge 0 \implies \RobotApproachVelocity \le 0\). Halting a moving system instantaneously would require infinite deceleration and unbounded torque (\(\ActuatorEffort = -\infty\)). Applying this first-derivative condition to position alone provides no torque constraint before the geometric boundary. Higher-order barrier formulations can retain a position constraint and differentiate until the input appears; this chapter instead incorporates the stopping distance explicitly.

To restore nonzero control authority (\(\LieDerivativeAlongControl \neq \ZeroVector\)), the barrier certificate must explicitly incorporate kinetic energy and braking limits. For an approaching arm joint, let \(\AngularClearance = \ToolClearanceDistance / \LeverArmLength\) represent the remaining angular clearance, \(\JointAngularSpeed > 0\) the approach speed, \(\EffectiveJointInertia \JointAngularAcceleration = \CommandedJointTorque + \JointBiasTorque\) the joint dynamics, and \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration\) a validated lower bound on net available angular braking deceleration. Adopting the linear class-\(\Glyph{\mathcal{K}}\) function \(\ClassKFunction(\BarrierFunction) = \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction\), where \(\LinearBarrierDecayRate > 0\) is a constant approach-rate gain, the kinetic barrier certificate is: \[\BarrierFunction(\AngularClearance,\JointAngularSpeed)=\AngularClearance-\frac{\JointAngularSpeed^2}{2\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration}. \tag{5}\] Differentiating \(\BarrierFunction(\AngularClearance,\JointAngularSpeed)\) along the joint dynamics yields \(\BarrierFunctionRate = \LieDerivativeAlongDrift + \LieDerivativeAlongControl\,\CommandedJointTorque\), where \(\LieDerivativeAlongControl = -\frac{\JointAngularSpeed}{\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration} \ne 0\) for any nonzero approach speed \(\JointAngularSpeed > 0\). Because commanded torque \(\CommandedJointTorque\) appears directly in this first time derivative with a nonzero coefficient, the kinetic barrier achieves relative degree one (\(\BarrierRelativeDegree = 1\)). Actuator commands now exert immediate control authority across the causal boundary. Enforcing the forward-invariance condition \(\BarrierFunctionRate + \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction \ge 0\) isolates the admissible torque into an explicit affine half-space constraint:7 \[\CommandedJointTorque\le-\JointBiasTorque+\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration\left(\frac{\LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction}{\JointAngularSpeed}-1\right),\qquad -\JointTorqueLimit\le\CommandedJointTorque\le\JointTorqueLimit. \tag{6}\] In this sign convention, positive angular velocity \(\JointAngularSpeed\), positive gravitational bias \(\JointBiasTorque\), and positive motor torque \(\CommandedJointTorque\) all act downward toward the workstation so that clearance shrinks at rate \(\AngularClearanceRate = -\JointAngularSpeed\), and a negative upper bound on \(\CommandedJointTorque\) in equation 6 therefore sets a minimum magnitude on the upward braking torque \(-\CommandedJointTorque\) that the motor must apply against gravity. This condition defines the linear half-space \(\BarrierConstraintNormal(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlInput \le \BarrierConstraintBound(\MechanicalPlantState)\) solved by the control barrier function quadratic program (CBF-QP) filter developed next, with the half-space normal and scalar threshold determined directly by the Jacobian-vector products: \(\BarrierConstraintNormal(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top = -\LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState)\) and \(\BarrierConstraintBound(\MechanicalPlantState) = \LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\MechanicalPlantState) + \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\).
