At standstill (\(\JointAngularSpeed = 0\)), the microcontroller firmware applies a separate static acceleration and position-hold check to prevent division by zero.8

7 Kinetic barrier directional derivative chain rule: Evaluating \(\BarrierFunctionRate(\AngularClearance, \JointAngularSpeed) = \frac{\partial \BarrierFunction}{\partial \AngularClearance}\AngularClearanceRate + \frac{\partial \BarrierFunction}{\partial \JointAngularSpeed}\JointAngularAcceleration\) with \(\AngularClearanceRate = -\JointAngularSpeed\) and \(\JointAngularAcceleration = (\CommandedJointTorque + \JointBiasTorque)/\EffectiveJointInertia\) yields \(\BarrierFunctionRate = (-\JointAngularSpeed - \frac{\JointAngularSpeed\JointBiasTorque}{\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration}) - \frac{\JointAngularSpeed}{\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration}\CommandedJointTorque = \LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\AngularClearance,\JointAngularSpeed) + \LieDerivativeAlongControl(\AngularClearance,\JointAngularSpeed)\CommandedJointTorque\). Substituting into \(\BarrierFunctionRate + \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction \ge 0\) and rearranging produces \(\frac{\JointAngularSpeed(\CommandedJointTorque + \JointBiasTorque)}{\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration} \le \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction - \JointAngularSpeed\). Multiplying by the positive factor \(\frac{\EffectiveJointInertia\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration}{\JointAngularSpeed}\) and subtracting the bias yields equation 6.

8 Standstill velocity thresholding: In microcontroller firmware, floating-point evaluation of \(\frac{\LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction}{\JointAngularSpeed}\) is explicitly guarded by a velocity threshold \(\StandstillSpeedThreshold\): when \(|\JointAngularSpeed| \le \StandstillSpeedThreshold\), the kinetic barrier is bypassed in favor of a static acceleration or position-hold bound, preventing floating-point division by zero and NaN exceptions in the sub-millisecond loop.

Applying this kinetic barrier to the descending-arm parameters from section 12.3 sets concrete torque ceilings across the approach. The moment of inertia is \(\EffectiveJointInertia =\) 3 kg·m². The gravitational bias torque is \(\JointBiasTorque =\) 65.25 N·m, directed toward the workstation. The actuator saturation limit is \(|\CommandedJointTorque| \le\) 87 N·m, so the net angular braking deceleration is \(\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration = (\JointTorqueLimit - \JointBiasTorque) / \EffectiveJointInertia =\) 7.25 \(\text{rad/s}^2\). The link lever arm is \(\LeverArmLength =\) 0.80 m, the approach speed is \(\JointAngularSpeed =\) 1.2 \(\text{rad/s}\), and the barrier gain is \(\LinearBarrierDecayRate =\) 10 \(\text{s}^{-1}\). These are stipulated joint-level dynamics that deployment must calibrate on the actual arm. At 110 mm of tool clearance from the workstation surface, the angular clearance is \(\AngularClearance =\) 0.1375 \(\text{rad}\) and the barrier certificate evaluates to \(\BarrierFunction =\) 0.0382 \(\text{rad}\), so substituting into equation 6 requires the joint to deliver at least 80.1 N·m of counteracting braking torque. Most of that demand is not deceleration at all, because the first 65.25 N·m merely cancels gravity to keep the joint from speeding up and only the remainder slows it, which is why a joint still outside its zero set already asks for most of its peak torque. At 79.45 mm, the system reaches the zero set (\(\BarrierFunction = 0\)) where the barrier demands 87 N·m, the motor’s full peak torque capacity. If clearance degrades further to 70 mm, the inequality demands 89.1 N·m, which exceeds peak actuator limits and places the state outside the control-admissible viable set. There, under these scalar dynamics, fixed bias, and instantaneous maximum-braking assumptions, no controller initiated at that point can prevent contact.

Inside 79.45 mm of clearance the barrier asks for more braking than the joint has.

Physical robotic hardware provides neither perfectly continuous state feedback nor instantaneous torque response. Stator current rise times, fieldbus jitter, and drivetrain mechanical compliance delay and soften corrective torque delivery. To guarantee safety under these real-world non-idealities, the runtime enforcer evaluates the barrier certificate against an inset boundary that explicitly accounts for the worst-case stopping deficit induced by hardware latency and compliant dynamics. This initiates corrective filtering earlier along the trajectory.

This kinetic barrier construction follows directly from rigid-body braking mechanics. For the stipulated scalar approach with exact dynamics, fixed bias, instantaneous effort, and no omitted margins, its zero level set represents the exact boundary of states from which maximum allowable braking stops the joint before collision: the viability kernel (Aubin 1991) of a double integrator subject to actuator saturation. Among collision-free states, the kernel’s complement is the set of inevitable collision states defined in Inevitable collision states. With a conservative available lower bound instead of exact maximum braking, the same construction supplies a sufficient stopping set rather than the exact kernel. The resulting torque requirement remains compatible with the stipulated actuator limits. At \(\BarrierFunction = 0\), the inequality in equation 6 demands exactly \(-\JointTorqueLimit\), and for all \(\BarrierFunction > 0\) the demand is strictly less restrictive, ensuring that at least one admissible control command exists everywhere within the safe set. Actuator feasibility across the entire set, not merely the calculus of the derivative, is what qualifies \(\BarrierFunction(\AngularClearance, \JointAngularSpeed)\) as a valid barrier certificate under input constraints.

This identical principle governs autonomous base navigation. The mobile base certificate \(\MobileBaseBarrierFunction(\ClearDistance, \SpeedAtDetection) = \ClearDistance - [\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay + \ResidentStopDistance(\SpeedAtDetection) + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin + \TrackingErrorBound]\) is nonnegative precisely when the stopping subtotal in equation 1 fits the clearance. The operational certificate must additionally subtract the dispatch-to-admission travel before granting permission. Both constructions reserve the motion needed to stop. A nonnegative stopping reservation alone does not establish forward invariance; the sampled implementation must also preserve that reservation under its qualified state, effort, and timing bounds. The single-joint arm derivation omits latency and localization margins temporarily only to isolate the mechanics of relative degree.

When complex nonlinear dynamics prevent closed-form analytical solutions for the viability kernel, Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability9 computes the boundary numerically as the zero level set of an optimal control value function (Mitchell et al. 2005; Bansal et al. 2017). The resulting tabulated lookup surface serves as the runtime barrier certificate, with its spatial gradient defining the half-space normal for real-time filtering. Because state-space grid discretization scales exponentially with dimensionality, HJ reachability lookup tables are best suited for decoupled, low-dimensional subsystems such as a single revolute joint or the longitudinal tracking channel of a mobile base.

9 Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability: Originating in the zero-sum differential game formulations of Isaacs (1965), reachability analysis frames safety as a game between a control policy and a worst-case adversarial disturbance. The resulting safe set contains precisely those states from which the controller can guarantee safety against any bounded disturbance trajectory. Consequently, an HJ reachability table is inherently robust but conservative, and its safety guarantee holds only within the assumed disturbance envelope.

Isaacs, Rufus. 1965. Differential Games: A Mathematical Theory with Applications to Warfare and Pursuit, Control and Optimization. Wiley.
Bansal, Somil, Mo Chen, Sylvia Herbert, and Claire J. Tomlin. 2017. “Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability: A Brief Overview and Recent Advances.” 2017 IEEE 56th Annual Conference on Decision and Control (CDC), 2242–53. https://doi.org/10.1109/CDC.2017.8263977.

↪ The Hamilton-Jacobi value function and its differential-game formulation are developed in Hamilton-Jacobi reachability and worst-case differential games.

A barrier certificate specifies which actuator commands preserve forward invariance, but it does not specify how the runtime safety path should handle an uncertified proposal. When a candidate command violates the half-space constraint, the enforcer must choose whether to project the command onto the boundary of admissible inputs, reject it in favor of a precomputed emergency braking trajectory, or maintain the previously certified setpoint. This intervention policy determines how mathematical barrier constraints translate into real-time authority over the motor drives.

12.5 Refusing Proposals and Enforcing State Invariants

A trajectory chunk arrives from the application processor on schedule, properly framed, and bearing a fresh evidence timestamp, yet one of its constituent joint setpoints would accelerate the arm directly into a steel rack upright. Nothing in the incoming packet payload marks that setpoint as erroneous. The permission path therefore grants zero executive authority to an incoming learned command until that proposal passes the configured state, input, freshness, and stopping-clearance checks. This is the third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), executed deterministically one 1 ms tick at a time. Rather than assuming that a learned proposal satisfies stability criteria, actuator limits, or stopping guarantees, the permission path treats every incoming vector strictly as an advisory candidate.

The resident controller and output selector translate this principle of refusal into deterministic execution. The resident controller maintains a fallback response qualified for the current machine state, while the selector determines whether to admit an incoming candidate, apply a feasible correction, or transfer authority directly to that resident response. Rejecting an incoming candidate does not instantaneously abort the command currently driving the plant. Instead, the active command continues running so long as its own evidence timestamp, lease deadline, tracking error bound, and stopping reservation remain valid.

Under this architecture, the upstream neural policy never commands the physical plant directly. Its proposals reach the safety MCU through the board link and seqlock mailbox, with no addressable memory shared between the two compute domains. Each admitted chunk drives the plant only under a chunk lease, a hardware-enforced dead-man permission10 that lapses unless a fresh, validated proposal renews it. A trajectory chunk arrives as a horizon of \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) waypoints, and the admission filter inspects all \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) points against reach, velocity, and stopping-reservation constraints before permitting the first waypoint to execute. On each subsequent control tick, the enforcer re-evaluates the specific setpoint scheduled for execution against live sensor telemetry. If the enforcer projects a setpoint on a single tick, it does not rewrite the chunk’s downstream waypoints. Instead, the safety MCU reports the intervention upstream, requiring the policy to replan from the actual, corrected state rather than resuming execution along a trajectory computed for a state the machine never reached.

10 Dead-Man Control: Early electric locomotives incorporated a spring-loaded dead-man handle that the operator had to depress continuously; releasing the handle (if the driver lost consciousness, for example) interrupted tractive power and engaged the mechanical brakes. System safety arose from the cessation of action rather than the transmission of an explicit fault message. The chunk lease applies the same principle in software. The host must actively renew its permission, ensuring that an upstream processor too damaged to command a stop is inherently incapable of renewing execution authority.

↩︎ The seqlock mailbox that hands proposals to the permission path is established in Lock-free and wait-free mailbox exchanges.

If the proposal remains fresh, the physical machine state resides within the qualified stopping region, and the tracking error satisfies \(\|\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant)\| \le \TrackingErrorBound\), the output selector permits the proposal (or its minimally projected variant \(\CertifiedControlAction\)) to drive the actuators. Conversely, if the chunk lease expires without renewal, tracking error breaches the tolerance bound, no admissible command satisfies instantaneous physical constraints, or the active command endangers physical boundaries beyond the corrective capacity of projection, the output selector revokes permission and unilaterally engages the baseline safety controller. The upstream neural policy is not consulted, cannot negotiate, and possesses no hardware mechanism to override the selector’s decision.

When a proposal conflicts with physical constraints, the enforcer chooses between filtering and vetoing the command. Filtering applies when the proposal is structurally sound and a nearby admissible control action preserves the policy’s underlying intent. Consider the manipulator arm approaching a spring-latched cage door when the chunk policy proposes approaching the strike plate at 0.10 m/s. The door zone admits only the guarded approach speed of 0.03 m/s defined by the target operational design domain (ODD), the velocity envelope validated across empirical trials and demonstration datasets. The faster proposal therefore fails on both evidential and physical grounds. It fails on evidence, because no trial or demonstration sampled that speed. It also fails on physics, because the contact analysis of section 12.6.2 shows that at that speed the force on the plate at brake onset nearly reaches the latch limit, leaving a stop that demands far harder arm deceleration than the guarded approach needs. Consequently, the enforcer preserves the approach trajectory’s target and spatial direction, projecting only its scalar speed down to the 0.03 m/s limit. The least-change quadratic program formulated in section 12.6 solves this projection in real time alongside the half-space constraints derived by a control barrier function.

↩︎ The evaluation record’s target ODD boundary, sampled at the guarded approach speed, is established in The Evaluation Record.

Filtering must account for momentum alongside position, ensuring that the actuators retain sufficient control authority to bring the machine to a complete stop before available clearance vanishes (section 12.4). In the latch example, scalar speed projection preserves both the goal and the approach direction. In a coupled joint-torque filter, however, a correction can alter the end-effector trajectory through task space; such modification remains permissible only when the revised motion satisfies dynamic continuity constraints and remains task-admissible. Conversely, a structurally invalid proposal (a discontinuous jump in commanded position, an acceleration profile beyond peak motor torque, or a trajectory pointed directly into an immovable obstacle) indicates that perception or inference has entered an unmodeled failure mode. Projecting an ungrounded candidate onto the nearest admissible boundary would execute arbitrary motion unrelated to any coherent task intent. Under these conditions, the enforcer exercises an outright veto. The selector permits the active command to finish only if that command continues to satisfy its own validity checks; otherwise, it immediately transfers authority to a prequalified local fallback.

Both filtering and veto operate strictly at runtime; offline training cannot substitute for either mechanism. Penalty terms, barrier losses, and reward shaping bias the policy distribution during training, but they cannot mathematically bound what the deployed neural network will emit in edge cases. The statistical safety fallacy conflates a bound on expected violation cost with an absolute guarantee across every execution trace. A constrained reinforcement learning objective such as \(\mathbb{E}[\text{cost}] \le \ExpectedCostLimit\) constrains only the ensemble mean, leaving open the possibility of catastrophic tail events during individual rollouts. Formal neural-network verification can establish provable output bounds over bounded input sets (Katz et al. 2017), and closed-loop verification can combine those bounds with plant models. Formal proofs nevertheless remain vulnerable to reality gaps: unmodeled friction, sensor latency, non-deterministic scheduling, or actuator saturation outside the proof’s operational assumptions will invalidate the guarantee. The permission path therefore verifies every candidate control vector at the hardware actuation boundary, regardless of the optimization objective used during training. When an incoming proposal is safe, the verification check is transparent, allowing learned sensorimotor competence to pass directly to the motor drives without alteration.

Katz, Guy, Clark Barrett, David L. Dill, Kyle Julian, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2017. “Reluplex: An Efficient SMT Solver for Verifying Deep Neural Networks.” International Conference on Computer Aided Verification (CAV), 97–117. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-63387-9_5.

↩︎ Why training-time penalties cannot bound the actions a deployed policy emits is established in Fallacies and Pitfalls.

The chunk lease, 60 ms on this machine, bounds upstream execution authority during a host stall or scheduling delay, provided the local timer, selector, sensing, and actuation paths survive. In addition to the lease, a dedicated 100 \(\text{Hz}\) host heartbeat monitors basic link viability with a 30 ms silence timeout. The safety MCU executes its deterministic 1 kHz control loop, servicing a local 5 ms hardware watchdog timer only after successfully completing an entire cycle; an independent hardware trip circuit supervises MCU power and clock integrity. If either the host heartbeat or the chunk lease expires, the safety MCU revokes proposal permissions and engages a deterministic, state-matched fallback from the responses that section 12.8 defines, assuming reserved clearance and actuator braking capacities remain sufficient. On the warehouse mobile manipulator, if a proposer renews its lease and subsequently stalls, mechanical braking begins within 82 ms of the last valid renewal. This lease-expiry scenario assumes the underlying heartbeat remains active even as valid proposals cease; a total host operating-system freeze trips the 30 ms heartbeat timeout even earlier. If a worker steps out from behind a warehouse rack, the sensor capture latency of the camera frame that first revealed the obstacle adds directly to this renewal-to-onset delay, and section 12.2 carries this cumulative latency directly into the admission check.

↩︎ The lease, heartbeat, and self-watchdog timers, and why a heartbeat cannot renew a lease, are established in Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts.

Severing upstream authority requires an unambiguously defined physical response at the lowest hardware interface. The zero-command floor defines the default physical state enforced when active software control terminates, but commanding ‘zero’ produces drastically different dynamics depending on the actuator topology. Injecting zero current into a low-friction, direct-drive actuator allows a gravity-loaded manipulator arm to drop freely unless a mechanical brake or counterweight holds the link. Conversely, commanding zero velocity to a stiff position-controlled servo can demand infinite acceleration and trigger violent inertial transients. As detailed in Unpowered states and unmanaged fallback dynamics, an unpowered drive drops into one of three hardware states: unconstrained coasting, phase-short dynamic braking, or mechanical locking via spring-applied brakes. The system architect must ensure that the commanded fallback matches both the instantaneous kinetic state and the physical capabilities of the surviving hardware.

Proposal rejection is a continuous, steady-state operating decision rather than an exceptional emergency trip. The enforcer must evaluate every invariant and execute its selected fallback deterministically even if the upstream proposer crashes completely, provided local sensing, braking authority, and stopping clearance remain intact. When those physical invariants hold, runtime filtering smoothly reconciles learned proposals with safety boundaries, preserving admissible task progress within the qualified physical envelope.

12.6 The Minimal Intervention Principle and Optimization Filters

When the arm approaches the cage-door latch too quickly while still proposing a useful target and direction, rejecting every such proposal would discard useful learned behavior, whereas accepting it unchecked would exceed the permitted approach speed. The minimal intervention principle resolves this trade-off by passing an admissible proposal untouched and choosing the smallest feasible correction, one that satisfies physical constraints and finishes before the permission deadline, whenever a proposal crosses a boundary. The resulting safety shield11 preserves useful task progress within the physical limits. For the torque forbidden by equation 6, the correction should remove only the constraint violation; replacing every near-boundary command with a stop would discard admissible learned behavior.

11 Shielding: In safe reinforcement learning, a shield sits between the learning agent and its environment and overrides only those actions that would violate a temporal-logic safety specification (Alshiekh et al. 2018). The CBF-QP is a continuous-control shield. Its override is the smallest torque change rather than a substituted discrete action, and its specification is a stopping margin rather than a logic formula, which lets it keep the learned action’s direction instead of replacing it.

Alshiekh, Mohammed, Roderick Bloem, Rüdiger Ehlers, Bettina Könighofer, Scott Niekum, and Ufuk Topcu. 2018. “Safe Reinforcement Learning via Shielding.” Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence 32. https://doi.org/10.1609/aaai.v32i1.11797.
Ames, Aaron D., Xiangru Xu, Jessy W. Grizzle, and Paulo Tabuada. 2017. “Control Barrier Function Based Quadratic Programs for Safety Critical Systems.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 62 (8): 3861–76. https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.2016.2638961.

For the already-derived torque half-space, the enforcer solves a constrained least-change problem, the CBF-QP safety filter (Ames et al. 2017). The tracker supplies the nominal effort; the barrier and actuator limits constrain the result. The combined program of Control Lyapunov functions and stabilization, which optimizes convergence and safety together and relaxes convergence when they conflict, is not needed here. The tracker of The Tracker Handoff and Dynamic Reference Envelopes already supplies the nominal effort, and the filter has only to keep it inside the barrier: \[\min_{\ControlInput \in \mathbb{R}^\ControlInputDimension} \frac{1}{2}\|\ControlInput - \NominalControlCommand\|^2 \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \BarrierConstraintNormal(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top \ControlInput \le \BarrierConstraintBound(\MechanicalPlantState), \quad \ActuatorTorqueLowerBound \le \ControlInput \le \ActuatorTorqueUpperBound \tag{7}\] where the half-space normal and threshold are defined by the directional derivatives \(\BarrierConstraintNormal(\MechanicalPlantState)^\top = -\LieDerivativeAlongControl(\MechanicalPlantState)\) and \(\BarrierConstraintBound(\MechanicalPlantState) = \LieDerivativeAlongDrift(\MechanicalPlantState) + \ClassKFunction(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState))\) derived in section 12.4.1, matching the single-axis torque constraint of equation 6, and \([\ActuatorTorqueLowerBound, \ActuatorTorqueUpperBound]\) represents the verified actuator torque envelope. The selected implementation allocates 150 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) to this solve. Dedicated memory and a fixed iteration cap support that target; qualification must establish completion or a timely fallback under the admitted constraint set.

12.6.1 Closed-form quadratic programming and torque projection

Clamping each joint axis independently, or projecting a command onto a barrier and then clipping it to motor torque limits afterward, can leave the coupled stopping constraint violated while skewing the direction of motion across task space. A single active barrier admits a closed-form projection when the projected command also lies inside the actuator bounds; if a bound becomes active, the enforcer must solve for the intersection rather than project onto the barrier and clamp afterward.

For a nonzero constraint normal and inactive actuator bounds, the single barrier constraint \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \ControlInput \le \BarrierConstraintBound\) in equation 7 admits the closed-form projection derived in Minimal-intervention quadratic program: \[\CertifiedControlAction = \NominalControlCommand - \BarrierProjectionMultiplier^* \BarrierConstraintNormal, \qquad \BarrierProjectionMultiplier^* = \max\left(0, \frac{\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \NominalControlCommand - \BarrierConstraintBound}{\|\BarrierConstraintNormal\|^2}\right) \tag{8}\] where the optimal dual multiplier \(\BarrierProjectionMultiplier^* \ge 0\) is zero whenever the nominal proposal is safe (\(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \NominalControlCommand \le \BarrierConstraintBound\)), leaving \(\CertifiedControlAction = \NominalControlCommand\). It is strictly positive when the proposal breaches the boundary (\(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \NominalControlCommand > \BarrierConstraintBound\)). In that case it scales the minimal intervention vector \(\FiniteChange \ControlInput = -\BarrierProjectionMultiplier^* \BarrierConstraintNormal\), which projects \(\CertifiedControlAction\) orthogonally onto the safe half-space boundary \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \ControlInput = \BarrierConstraintBound\).

On a two-axis manipulator with illustrative values, suppose the tracker following setpoints from an unconstrained neural policy computes nominal joint torques \(\NominalControlCommand = [\) 3.5, 3 \(]^\top\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}\) against a validated safety half-space \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \ControlInput \le \BarrierConstraintBound\). Its parameters are \(\BarrierConstraintNormal = [\) 2, 1 \(]^\top\) and \(\BarrierConstraintBound =\) 5 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\). The per-joint actuator limits are ±5 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\). Because the candidate gives \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \NominalControlCommand =\) 10 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\) and breaches the boundary by 5 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\), equation 8 yields \(\BarrierProjectionMultiplier^* =\) 1 and projects the command to \(\CertifiedControlAction = [\) 1.5, 2 \(]^\top\text{ N}\cdot\text{m}\). This meets the half-space exactly while keeping both joints within their limits at an intervention norm of 2.236 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\). By contrast, an independent per-axis clamp that ignores the coupling and cuts only the joint with the larger normal component holds Joint 1 to 2 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\) while passing Joint 2 at 3 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\). That clamp gives \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \ClampedControlCommand =\) 7 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\), still 2 \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}\) outside the constraint, while skewing the torque vector from 40.6\(^\circ\) to 56.3\(^\circ\). The per-axis clamp both violates the coupled constraint and distorts the command. Orthogonal projection satisfies the constraint with the smallest possible torque change, though it too rotates the torque vector, so the resulting end-effector path still requires checking against the coupled plant model.

This geometric behavior exposes the distinction between torque-space and task-space minimal intervention: an orthogonal projection \(\CertifiedControlAction = \NominalControlCommand + \FiniteChange \ControlInput\) minimizes joint effort deviation \(\|\ControlInput - \NominalControlCommand\|^2\), which redistributes multi-axis joint torques and rotates the net Cartesian wrench. While a feasible projection can preserve the safe set \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) under the stated model and timing assumptions, it deflects the end-effector trajectory and may arrest task progress. If the upstream neural policy continues emitting unprojected nominal setpoints without awareness of the intervention, the robot can chatter or stall at the boundary. The downstream enforcer must therefore stream the intervention vector \(\FiniteChange \ControlInput\) back to the application processor as a telemetry signal, notifying the high-level planner to re-plan around the newly active physical constraint.

The same correction has a picture in state space and one in torque space (figure 7). In state space, a trajectory that would breach the velocity-dependent boundary \(\SafetyBoundaryManifold\) is turned along it; in the torque plane, the projection places \(\CertifiedControlAction\) on the half-space boundary \(\BarrierConstraintNormal^\top \ControlInput = \BarrierConstraintBound\), while the per-axis clamp leaves \(\ClampedControlCommand\) outside it and skews its direction.

Filter choice joins a mathematical condition to a measured execution budget and the machine’s available braking authority. As summarized in table 2, architectural choices differ in their handling of relative degree, solver worst-case execution time (WCET), forward invariance guarantees, and directional fidelity. Independent clamping can miss coupled constraints; a CBF-QP can enforce a derived half-space if it is feasible and finishes within the verified cycle. Predictive filters (Wabersich and Zeilinger 2021) trade a longer horizon for configuration-dependent compute cost.

Wabersich, Kim P., and Melanie N. Zeilinger. 2021. “A Predictive Safety Filter for Learning-Based Control of Constrained Nonlinear Dynamical Systems.” Automatica 129: 109647.
Gurriet, Thomas, Mark Mote, Aaron D. Ames, and Eric Feron. 2018. “An Online Approach to Active Set Invariance.” 2018 IEEE Conference on Decision and Control (CDC), 3592–99. https://doi.org/10.1109/CDC.2018.8619139.
Chen, Yuxiao, Mrdjan Jankovic, Mario Santillo, and Aaron D. Ames. 2021. “Backup Control Barrier Functions: Formulation and Comparative Study.” 2021 60th IEEE Conference on Decision and Control (CDC), 6835–41. https://doi.org/10.1109/CDC45484.2021.9683111.
Fisac, Jaime F., Anayo K. Akametalu, Melanie N. Zeilinger, Shahab Kaynama, Jeremy Gillula, and Claire J. Tomlin. 2019. “A General Safety Framework for Learning-Based Control in Uncertain Robotic Systems.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 64 (7): 2737–52. https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.2018.2876389.

The rows of table 2 belong to one unified family rather than rival techniques (Hsu et al. 2024): each filter monitors a candidate against a fallback policy known to keep the machine safe and intervenes whenever the candidate would leave the set from which that fallback still works, differing only in how the intervention occurs. Whereas a least-restrictive filter passes the proposal untouched up to the boundary and then switches abruptly to the fallback (which is simple to implement but chatters along the boundary), a smooth filter such as the CBF-QP blends in a correction before the boundary is reached. This chapter’s enforcer combines both mechanisms: admitting a trajectory only with a matched stop suffix and holding a state-matched fallback for every reachable state forms a backup-set filter that checks safety by rolling the fallback forward from the proposed state (Gurriet et al. 2018; Chen et al. 2021). The per-tick CBF-QP acts as the smooth local filter layered on top of it, and HJ reachability (Fisac et al. 2019) supplies the value function when a precomputed surface replaces a closed-form barrier.

Operating near an active safe-set boundary can produce chattering. If a learned policy repeatedly proposes an unsafe command, sampled enforcement may switch the active constraint across successive 1 ms ticks. Depending on the plant and controller, rapid torque changes can excite a structural mode or increase motor heating and transmission wear. A tested boundary layer or hysteresis can reduce switching, but its added tracking error must fit inside the validated stopping margin.

The intervention vector \(\FiniteChange \ControlInput = \CertifiedControlAction - \NominalControlCommand\) also measures upstream policy health. A rising intervention frequency or magnitude can indicate proposal drift, a changed task, or a changed plant. Normal filtering can also preserve successful whole-machine service while reducing unassisted proposer autonomy; the log must distinguish these outcomes rather than count every correction as a task failure. Summed over an illustrative rolling window of \(\InterventionWindowDuration =\) 500 ms as \(\sum_{k} \|\CertifiedControlAction_k - {\NominalControlCommand}_k\| \FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), the metric lets the permission path flag divergence for logging or operator notification while the per-tick limits still protect the plant.

12.6.2 Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers

Once the manipulator arm reaches the cage-door strike plate (\(\AngularClearance = 0\)), the spatial kinetic barrier (equation 5) reduces to \(\BarrierFunction(0, \JointAngularSpeed) = -\JointAngularSpeed^2 / (2\NetAngularBrakingDeceleration) \ge 0\), which admits only zero speed (\(\JointAngularSpeed = 0\)) and freezes the gripper before it can depress the latch. A spatial kinetic barrier operates under the premise that the robot’s objective is to avoid collision (\(\AngularClearance > 0\)), whereas manipulation, grasping, and assembly require intentional physical contact (\(\AngularClearance = 0\)). While the spatial barrier can admit arrival at contact with zero approach speed, it does not govern the subsequent force-controlled interaction where geometric separation no longer supplies the relevant margin. Once the warehouse mobile manipulator’s gripper meets the strike plate at the guarded approach, the arm must push against the latch spring, and the margin that matters is the energy and force it delivers there.

Enforcing safety during intentional contact requires transitioning from a spatial clearance barrier to an energetic passivity barrier. When physical clearance is zero (\(\ClearDistance = 0\)), safety cannot mean zero velocity; it means bounding mechanical energy exchange and normal contact force \(\NormalContactForce\). A controller in physical contact interacts with an unmodeled, potentially stiff environment. If tracking errors or neural policy oscillations pump unmeasured energy into the interface, the closed-loop system undergoes contact chattering, limit cycles, or high-force impact spikes that damage both the workpiece and the robot’s transmission.

To bound active energy exchange, the enforcer augments the controller state with an energy tank \(\EnergyTankLevel(\TimeInstant)\)12 (Ferraguti et al. 2013; Hogan 1985) grounded in passivity. A passive system can return no more energy than its initial stored energy plus the energy it absorbs, so a power-preserving interconnection with a passive environment retains a passive energy balance (while a stability claim additionally requires the appropriate dissipation and state-observability conditions). The tank enforces that property by bookkeeping, acting as a software tally of work rather than a physical store of energy. Every joule that virtual damping removes from the arm’s motion is credited to the tank, and the controller may spend actively only what the tank holds, namely its accumulated credits plus a fixed initial reserve. In this contact formulation, \(\ActiveControlTorque\) denotes the active torque component excluding damping, so the total controller torque is \(\ActiveControlTorque-\VirtualDampingMatrix\GeneralizedVelocities\). The damping term can credit the tank only to the extent that the actuator actually realizes the corresponding dissipative work, and using total torque in the debit while also crediting damping would count the same dissipation twice. With those components separated, the modeled balance is: \[\EnergyTankPower = \GeneralizedVelocities^\top \VirtualDampingMatrix\,\GeneralizedVelocities - \ActiveControlTorque^\top \GeneralizedVelocities \tag{9}\] where \(\GeneralizedVelocities^\top \VirtualDampingMatrix\,\GeneralizedVelocities \ge 0\) is the dissipative power credited under qualified torque tracking and \(\ActiveControlTorque^\top \GeneralizedVelocities\) is active mechanical power. Saturation, delay, or tracking error requires conservative bounds on realized work rather than credit for an unrealized damping request. The enforcer then defines the energy-tank barrier certificate of equation 10: \[\EnergyTankBarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState, \EnergyTankLevel) = \EnergyTankLevel - \EnergyTankReserve \ge 0 \tag{10}\] where \(\EnergyTankReserve > 0\) is a reserved baseline. Combined with a hard contact-force limit \(\ContactForceBarrierFunction(\MeasuredContactForce) = \ContactForceLimit - \|\MeasuredContactForce\| \ge 0\), the enforcer establishes the in-contact viable set \(\InContactViableSet\). While \(\EnergyTankLevel > \EnergyTankReserve\) and \(\|\MeasuredContactForce\| < \ContactForceLimit\), candidate neural actions are admitted to perform manipulation. If the task consumes the allocated energy budget (\(\EnergyTankLevel \to \EnergyTankReserve\)) or contact force approaches \(\ContactForceLimit\), the barrier filter clamps active energy injection (\(\ActiveControlTorque^\top \GeneralizedVelocities \le 0\)), restricting active work under the modeled power balance. This energy constraint does not by itself bound contact force, because a passive interaction with a stiff surface can still produce a large force. The force channel needs its own sensing-error, response-delay, and actuator bounds before the combined constraints can justify continued contact.

12 Dissipativity: Willems (1972) generalized circuit passivity to any dynamical system with a storage function, an energy-like quantity that can grow no faster than the power supplied to the system. Interconnected passive systems inherit that bound, which is why contact with an unknown but passive environment can be kept stable without a model of the environment. The energy tank is a storage function that the controller keeps explicitly in software, so the bound holds only as far as the tank’s bookkeeping matches the work the actuators actually do.

Willems, Jan C. 1972. “Dissipative Dynamical Systems Part I: General Theory.” Archive for Rational Mechanics and Analysis 45 (5): 321–51. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00276493.

For a contact command, integrating the work balance in equation 9 over one control interval \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) supplies the energy allowance for the same least-change decision. Suppose the current tank level is \({\EnergyTankLevel}_0\ge \EnergyTankReserve\), a conservative lower bound on damping power actually realized is \(\RealizedDampingPower\), and a bounded contact-direction speed \(\ContactDirectionSpeed>0\) is held over this simplified interval. The spendable work is then \({\EnergyTankLevel}_0-\EnergyTankReserve+\RealizedDampingPower\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\). Because a proposed constant force \(\ProposedContactForce \ge 0\) debits \(\ProposedContactForce \ContactDirectionSpeed \FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), the energy allowance caps force at \(\EnergyLimitedContactForce=({\EnergyTankLevel}_0-\EnergyTankReserve+\RealizedDampingPower\FiniteChange \TimeInstant)/(\ContactDirectionSpeed\FiniteChange \TimeInstant)\). Letting \(\ContactForceCap\) denote the smaller of the deliverable force and the contact-force ceiling after sensing and delay allowances, the admitted command under these stipulated scalar conditions is \(\AdmittedContactForce = \min(\ProposedContactForce, \EnergyLimitedContactForce, \ContactForceCap)\), whose debit leaves \({\EnergyTankLevel}_1={\EnergyTankLevel}_0+\RealizedDampingPower\FiniteChange \TimeInstant-\AdmittedContactForce \ContactDirectionSpeed\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\ge \EnergyTankReserve\) while keeping force below the independent cap. The controller can therefore continue useful contact with a reduced force instead of abandoning the task. At the reserve with no credited damping, however, no positive active work is admitted. Zero speed requires a separate force check rather than division by speed, and delayed or uncertain power requires conservative work bounds over the interval.

The force channel’s own bounds can be computed for the running machine’s two contacts: the cage door, which the arm pushes on purpose, and the coworker’s hand, which it must never press too hard. Each calculation turns a force limit into a speed that the enforcement record carries as a channel ceiling, and each rests on premises that only a measurement can settle. They are derived here, where the channel is enforced; the release decision consumes the results as premises.

↪ Contact force and speed ceilings are enforced as release premises in Physical Criteria for Release.

At the door the quantity that matters is how fast force rises. Against a strike plate of stiffness \(\LatchPlateStiffness\), an arm approaching at \(\LatchApproachSpeed\) raises the contact force at \(d\NormalContactForce/d\TimeInstant = \LatchPlateStiffness\,\LatchApproachSpeed\), which at the guarded 0.03 m/s against the 4.0 × 10⁵ N/m plate is 12 N/ms. The contact tripwire fires at \(\ContactTripwireForce =\) 15 N, but the contact loop needs \(\ContactLoopResponseTime =\) 2 ms for detection, command delivery, and torque rise, and the plate keeps compressing by \(\LatchApproachSpeed \ContactLoopResponseTime\) before deceleration begins. The force at physical braking onset is therefore \[\BrakingOnsetContactForce = \ContactTripwireForce + \LatchPlateStiffness\,\LatchApproachSpeed\,\ContactLoopResponseTime \tag{11}\] which evaluates to 39 N, well inside the 100 N latch limit. The limit still binds the stop that follows, because the plate goes on compressing while the arm sheds its remaining kinetic energy: \[\MaximumPlateIndentation = \frac{\BrakingOnsetContactForce}{\LatchPlateStiffness} + \frac{\LatchApproachSpeed^2}{2\ArmStoppingDeceleration} \le \frac{\LatchForceLimit}{\LatchPlateStiffness} \tag{12}\] Against time since first contact (figure 8), force climbs through the tripwire and across the loop-response window to 39 N at brake onset; the commanded stop then bends the curve over to finish at the 100 N latch limit, while an unbraked run crosses the limit within a few milliseconds. The braking term ignores the plate’s spring work, which would help decelerate the arm, so the bound is conservative. Below the limit the plate can compress only 0.15 mm further, and stopping within that distance requires about 3 m/s², an illustrative stop of about 10 ms.

The same two equations show why the approach speed is an enforced channel rather than a preference. At the chunk policy’s proposed 0.10 m/s (section 12.5), equation 11 already gives 95 N at braking onset, and equation 12 then demands an arm deceleration of 400 m/s². The faster approach spends the response interval on force that the stop can no longer absorb. This is the physical half of the refusal in section 12.5; the evidential half is that no trial sampled this speed. Both results treat the arm as rigid and the plate as the whole contact stiffness. Gripper, wrist-sensor, and joint compliance in series lower the effective stiffness and slow the rise, while a geared joint reflects rotor inertia into the moving mass. Whether this arm achieves the required deceleration within a fraction of a millimeter depends on joint torque, drive rise time, and structural stiffness that only arm stopping trials establish. A compliant element placed in series on purpose turns that softening into passive protection. Springs in series add their compliances, so the combined stiffness falls below that of the softer element and the force rises more slowly, a protection that holds even when the enforcer cannot stop in time. The stiffness of that element is therefore part of the contact margin, and Responses when the state remains hidden sizes it for this latch.

The coworker’s hand is the opposite case, a contact the task tolerates only up to a bounded peak force. The force depends first on which contact occurs. In a transient contact the hand is free and recoils, so both bodies move and the collision acts through their reduced mass \(\ReducedImpactMass\). In a clamped (quasi-static) contact the hand is trapped against a rack upright or the robot’s own body, cannot recoil, and receives the full robot-side effective mass at the tool center point, \(\EffectiveImpactMass =\) 12 kg. The biomechanical body model of ISO/TS 15066:2016, whose collaborative-application requirements ISO 10218-2:2025 now incorporates (section 12.8.3), assigns the hand and fingers an effective mass \(\BodyRegionMass\) of 0.6 kg and an effective spring constant \(\HertzianContactStiffness\) of 75 N/mm (International Organization for Standardization 2016, 2025b; Kirschner et al. 2022). This worked example estimates the robot-side mass as half the arm’s moving mass plus its payload capacity; it does not compute the configuration- and direction-dependent mass reflected through the contact Jacobian. In an unbraked elastic contact the peak force is \[\UnbrakedImpactForce = \ContactApproachSpeed\,\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness\,\ReducedImpactMass}, \qquad \ReducedImpactMass = \left(\frac{1}{\BodyRegionMass} + \frac{1}{\EffectiveImpactMass}\right)^{-1} \tag{13}\] This is the spring-mass bound of equation 6.7, which equated a moving mass’s kinetic energy to the peak elastic energy of the contact, with one change: two bodies move. Momentum conservation keeps the pair’s common (center-of-mass) motion unchanged through the contact, so only the kinetic energy of their relative motion, \(\frac{1}{2}\ReducedImpactMass \ContactApproachSpeed^2\) for a closing speed \(\ContactApproachSpeed\), can compress the hand, and the reduced mass takes the place of the single moving mass. For this hand and arm, \(\ReducedImpactMass\) is 0.57 kg, because the light hand dominates the sum; a clamped hand is the limit \(\BodyRegionMass \to \infty\), in which \(\ReducedImpactMass\) becomes \(\EffectiveImpactMass\). Solved for speed at a force criterion \(\HandForceCriterion\), the equation gives the channel ceiling \(\ContactApproachSpeed = \HandForceCriterion/\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness\,\ReducedImpactMass}\). The peak follows the assumed masses and stiffness; the model establishes neither an impact duration nor a pressure or injury limit.

Kirschner, Robin Jeanne, Nico Mansfeld, Saeed Abdolshah, and Sami Haddadin. 2022. “ISO/TS 15066: How Different Interpretations Affect Risk Assessment.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2203.02706.

↩︎ The single-mass spring-impact bound that this reduced-mass form extends is established in The sim-to-real contact exploit.

At the arm’s 1 m/s free-space limit even the transient model predicts 207 N, or 4.1× the 50 N criterion, so the arm cannot enter an unseparated contact at that speed. At the handover speed the two contact cases bound the approach very differently (notebook 12.3).

Napkin Math 12.3: The clamped-hand penalty
Problem: The arm approaches the coworker’s hand at the handover speed. What peak force does equation 13 predict for a free hand that recoils and for a hand clamped against a rack upright, and what approach speed does the force criterion allow in each case?

Variables:

  • \(\EffectiveImpactMass =\) 12 kg (Derived; the simplified half-arm-mass plus payload-capacity estimate used above).
  • \(\BodyRegionMass =\) 0.6 kg and \(\HertzianContactStiffness =\) 75 N/mm (Datasheet; the ISO/TS 15066 hand body model).
  • \(\ContactApproachSpeed =\) 0.10 m/s (Chosen; the handover approach while the coworker holds the accept_item grant that Human Authority, Reaction Time, and Clearance defines).
  • \(\HandForceCriterion =\) 50 N (Chosen; the handover force criterion).

Math: For a free hand, \(\ReducedImpactMass =\) (1/0.6 + 1/12)\(^{-1} =\) 0.571 kg, and with \(\HertzianContactStiffness =\) 75,000 N/m the stiffness term is \(\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness\ReducedImpactMass} =\) 207 N per m/s. The peak force is \(\ContactApproachSpeed\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness\ReducedImpactMass} =\) 0.10 \(\times\) 207 \(=\) 20.7 N, and the criterion admits \(\HandForceCriterion/\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness\ReducedImpactMass} =\) 50 \(/\) 207, or 0.24 m/s. A clamped hand cannot recoil, so \(\ReducedImpactMass\) becomes \(\EffectiveImpactMass\) and \(\sqrt{\HertzianContactStiffness \EffectiveImpactMass} =\) 949 N per m/s. The same approach then delivers 0.10 \(\times\) 949 \(=\) 94.9 N, and the criterion admits only 50 \(/\) 949, or 0.053 m/s.

Result: The free hand sees 20.7 N, inside the criterion; the clamped hand sees 94.9 N, which fails it.

Systems insight: Clamping multiplies the force at any speed by \(\sqrt{\EffectiveImpactMass/\ReducedImpactMass} =\) 4.6, because the whole arm-side mass, not the light hand, sets the collision. A speed ceiling derived for a free hand is therefore a geometric premise as much as a kinematic one.

At one handover speed a free hand passes the force criterion and a clamped hand fails it.

The handover channel is therefore a transient-contact ceiling. It holds only while the handover geometry excludes clamping, with the handover point in free space and no rack upright, tote edge, or link of the arm within reach of the hand along the approach. Both contacts end the same way. The arithmetic is exact; what it cannot supply is the premise it rests on, whether an achievable arm deceleration, a contact stiffness, or a clamp-free geometry. The enforcement record keeps each ceiling beside that premise, and the same discipline governs every barrier in this chapter.

12.6.3 The conditions forward invariance rests on

Unmodeled friction loss, actuator saturation, solver overruns, or inter-sample drift under a held torque command can each push a physical plant across a barrier boundary even when the continuous-time inequality is satisfied at the sample instant. The containment claim provided by both spatial and energetic barrier calculations is therefore strictly conditional. A forward-invariance claim requires a feasible barrier and the following bounded conditions; the list is necessary for this design, not an exhaustive if-and-only-if test:

  1. Model Fidelity: The nominal plant dynamics \(\ControlAffineDriftField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) and \(\ControlAffineInputField(\MechanicalPlantState)\) must capture the true system dynamics within a validated parameter error bound.
  2. Disturbance Bounds: External forces and unmodeled friction acting on the mass must remain strictly within the modeled disturbance envelope \(|\ExternalDisturbanceForce(\TimeInstant)| \le \DisturbanceForceBound\).
  3. Actuation Authority: Physical actuators must possess sufficient voltage, current, and thermal headroom to realize the commanded corrective force within a qualified delivered-effort error and response-delay envelope, as well as without saturation (\(\ActuatorEffort^* \in [\MinimumActuatorEffort, \MaximumActuatorEffort]\)).
  4. Solver Determinism: The optimization solver or projection algorithm must terminate strictly within its allocated execution budget (\(\FilterSolveTime \le \FilterSolveBudget < \PermissionLoopPeriod\)).
  5. Discretization Invariance: The discrete sampling interval \(\PermissionLoopPeriod\) must be sufficiently small that inter-sample state drift does not breach the continuous-time invariance boundary.

The approach-acceleration upper bound is a separate premise from the lower bound on available braking, and equality of their illustrative magnitudes does not identify their roles. Each of the five conditions maps to a dedicated engineering budget: model fidelity and disturbance bounds come from system identification and bench load testing, actuation authority from dynamometer curves and thermal limits, solver determinism from static worst-case execution time analysis on the MCU, and discretization from the loop period itself. Because the drive holds the last admitted command between samples (a zero-order hold13), the plant drifts for one full period before the next check can act on it. The drift covers at most \(\FiniteChange \ZohHoldTravel = \BodySpeed \PermissionLoopPeriod + \frac{1}{2} \ProfileAccelerationBound \PermissionLoopPeriod^2\) at speed \(\BodySpeed\), where the worst case is the highest speed the base can reach rather than the ceiling \(\SpeedBound\) that admission enforces. At the 1.5 m/s drive limit, an independently stipulated 2 m/s² upper bound on approach acceleration, and \(\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1 ms, that drift is 1.501 mm, a small fraction of \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\). Tightening a raw-clearance check by this displacement preserves geometric separation over the hold interval under the stated motion bounds, whereas a kinetic or energy barrier requires a bound on its own evolution throughout the interval.

13 Zero-Order Hold: The order counts the derivatives the hold carries forward. A zero-order hold keeps the command’s value constant until the next sample, while a first-order hold would also extrapolate its slope. For one control tick the plant therefore integrates a torque the controller can no longer change, which is the source of the drift buffer \(\BodySpeed \PermissionLoopPeriod + \frac{1}{2}\ProfileAccelerationBound \PermissionLoopPeriod^2\); Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag develops the hold’s delay.

A condition that no detector watches is an assumption, so the permission path attaches one to each. A disturbance observer compares predicted with measured acceleration on every cycle. Inverter status registers and current shunts report actuator saturation. A hardware watchdog and cycle counter time the solver against its deadline. Timestamp counters on the sensor bus catch jitter and missed samples. When a detector invalidates a premise, the enforcer cannot restore permission by substituting optimistic parameters. It selects a response whose sensing, effort, and clearance premises survive, as developed in section 12.8. Both the free-space projection and contact decision must finish before the physical margin reserved for computation is spent.

12.6.4 Execution timing in the sub-millisecond loop

A correct barrier calculation delivered after the permission deadline cannot protect the margin reserved for that deadline. The whole cycle must therefore have a bounded execution time (Wilhelm et al. 2008), including state acquisition and the final drive transfer. A real-time operating system can reduce scheduling delays, but its name does not establish a bound on shared-memory or peripheral interference. The permission path’s implementation must be qualified under the competing workloads that can delay it, so the declared cycle allocation below is a timing requirement carried into loaded placement tests rather than a measured trace or a platform-independent solver guarantee.

In this heterogeneous design, the shield executes inside a 1 kHz timer interrupt routine on the MCU. An unloaded cycle of acquisition, validation, and a typical active-set solve completes in about 135 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), an illustrative estimate rather than a bound. The candidate path instead declares a worst-case execution-time requirement of 250 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) for the whole cycle, divided among the stages below, and the whole sequence must finish inside the 400 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) deadline:

The QP solve takes 150 of the 250 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) declared cycle, inside a 400 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) deadline.

  1. Interrupt Entry & State Acquisition (30 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): The hardware timer triggers the interrupt service routine (ISR), and firmware latches the wheel and joint encoder registers and the latest inertial measurement unit (IMU) sample.
  2. Proposal & Lease Verification (15 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): The MCU makes one bounded read of its local mailbox, which the link receiver fills outside this routine, validates the checksum, and confirms sequence order before checking evidence age, the active chunk lease, and the host heartbeat timer. In the evidence-age check, the evidence’s age at dispatch is the difference between the source frame’s evidence timestamp and the host dispatch timestamp in the proposal header (Supervisory timers and proposal wire contracts). That age may not exceed 51.8 ms, which combines the 51.6 ms observation age charged in row 5 of table 1 with 0.2 ms of clock uncertainty (The Observation Contract). Passing that test qualifies the evidence only at dispatch, so the MCU also reconstructs the evidence’s current age from its source epoch and elapsed time and checks it against the evidence’s own validity deadline and the remaining physical margin. Inference, admission, waiting, and transfer after dispatch must already be reserved once in that margin through the dispatch-to-admission allocation inside the timing allowance. Otherwise fresh evidence is required before activation, because a new proposal timestamp cannot renew old evidence. The MCU then verifies that elapsed time since valid admission or renewal has not exceeded the 60 ms active chunk lease and reads the host heartbeat timer, which marks the host silent once no heartbeat has arrived within the 30 ms timeout.
  3. Tracking Contract Verification (10 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): Firmware evaluates instantaneous tracking error norm \(\|\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant)\| = \|\CartesianPosition(\TimeInstant) - \ReferencePosition(\TimeInstant)\|\) against the qualified bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\).
  4. CBF-QP Safety Shield Solve (150 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): An active-set QP solver14 running in pre-allocated static SRAM, the MCU’s tightly coupled data memory, evaluates active barrier inequalities and computes \(\CertifiedControlAction = \arg\min_{\ControlInput \in \mathbb{R}^\ControlInputDimension} \frac{1}{2}\|\ControlInput - \NominalControlCommand\|^2\). A fixed iteration cap limits the work count; meeting the allocation also requires bounded per-iteration arithmetic, memory access, interrupt interference, and output service, including ill-conditioned constraint geometry. Dynamic memory allocation is forbidden on this path so that heap service is excluded from that timing obligation. In this two-processor design no memory is shared with the host, so camera direct memory access (DMA) streams cannot reach the memory this cycle uses. The couplings that remain are the link receiver, whose DMA writes into the MCU’s own memory, and any shared supply rail or clock; The Placement Record measures that residual interference and tests whether the isolation holds under load.
  5. Output Selection & Admitted Command Staging (15 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): If the QP is feasible and constraints hold, the enforcer emits the admitted command. The drives close the velocity and current loops. Because industrial servo drives running in position mode choose torque direction internally from position tracking error while an unsigned torque limit clips only the magnitude of each joint’s effort symmetrically, a position setpoint plus an unsigned torque limit does not by itself realize the signed braking effort or coupled multi-axis half-space the QP requires. This interface needs a qualified mapping from the staged command to delivered effort, with bounded realization error and response delay. The filter tightens each commanded half-space by the largest possible projection of that effort error along its normal, so every effort the drive may realize still satisfies the delivered-effort constraint. Near full braking, that allowance may remove feasibility and require earlier intervention. The drive configuration and qualification evidence must establish these bounds; this example supplies no measured error or delay. During the response interval, a previously held command must remain valid and the intersample/response allowance must cover its effect, charging each delay once. If the position/limit interface cannot satisfy this contract, the design needs a qualified effort interface or a stricter qualified operating envelope and fallback. The next position setpoint and per-axis torque limit travel in the next EtherCAT frame only under that realization contract. Drive-reported torque on the following tick can detect a breach but cannot retroactively certify the preceding interval; an invalid replacement leaves previously admitted setpoints in force only while their own evidence, lease, state, and stopping reservation remain valid.
  6. Completion & Reserve (30 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)): The MCU checks the register write and services its own watchdog only after the cycle succeeds. The reserve absorbs variation in the stages above; a cycle that has not completed by the deadline requires a qualified preempting timer or an independent mechanism to select the resident fallback before the next tick. The overrunning ISR cannot be assumed to detect and recover from its own stall. Only with that bounded transfer does a missed tick cost the base 1.3 mm of travel at the aisle speed. Empirical Verification of Silicon Isolation tests whether the declared WCET holds under load, so that ticks are not missed.

14 Active-Set QP: An active-set solver guesses which constraints hold with equality, solves the resulting linear system, and adds or drops one constraint per iteration. Dual variants such as that of Goldfarb and Idnani (1983) start from the unconstrained optimum, here the nominal command itself, and add only violated constraints, so a proposal that already satisfies every barrier costs almost nothing. The iteration count is bounded by the combinations of the constraint set rather than by a clock, so a fixed iteration cap bounds steps while the time per step must still be measured under interference.

Goldfarb, Donald, and Ashok Idnani. 1983. “A Numerically Stable Dual Method for Solving Strictly Convex Quadratic Programs.” Mathematical Programming 27 (1): 1–33. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02591962.

↪ The schedulability analysis that a declared WCET and deadline feed is developed in Real-Time Timers, Watchdogs, and Schedulability.

Stage 5 is also where refusal takes effect. Any of several conditions triggers it. The chunk lease may have expired, the host heartbeat may have fallen silent, the shared drive bus may report undervoltage, tracking error \(\|\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant)\|\) may exceed \(\TrackingErrorBound\), actuator saturation may render the feasible control set empty (\(\SafeControlSet = \emptyset\)), or an operating-contract detector such as the disturbance observer or the solver deadline may trip. In any of these cases, the output selector withholds the admitted command, revokes policy authority, and selects the state-matched stop. At standstill, lease or heartbeat expiry selects HOLD, the powered standstill hold that section 12.8 defines, only while powered support remains available. Undervoltage instead requires a qualified transition to mechanical restraint before that support expires. In motion, STOP under undervoltage remains conditional on drive survival.

Checkpoint 12.2: Barrier, projection, and cycle budget

The barrier fixes which commands preserve the stop; the filter and its cycle decide whether the chosen command arrives in time.

Every stage of this cycle assumes that the solve returns a feasible command. When saturation empties the admissible torque set, the lease lapses, or the cycle overruns its deadline, projection has nothing to return, and the permission path must already hold a response it can execute without the proposer. Executing that check deterministically while surviving proposer faults requires placing the enforcer where the host processor cannot preempt or bypass it.

12.7 Admission, Cyclic Checking, and Monitor Independence

A planned waypoint can clear a warehouse rack upright in the planner’s geometric check while the physical manipulator overshoots into the steel during execution, and an instantaneous motor torque well within electrical limits can accelerate the arm past the point where its remaining stopping reservation can arrest motion. Relying on trajectory admission alone misses in-flight tracking overshoot and saturation, whereas checking only single-step torque commands without a verified stopping continuation lets the arm enter an inevitable collision state before the local filter reacts. Robust safety enforcement therefore demands both layers (the per-tick cycle of section 12.6.4 on a dedicated MCU and full-plan admission before motion begins). That raises three structural questions: why neither layer can replace the other, where the cyclic checker sits relative to the command stream, and how independent of the proposer the MCU must actually be.

Full-plan admission judges the candidate trajectory, its seams, its swept clearance, and its verified stopping continuation before the machine commits to motion. The expensive geometry behind that judgment (queries of global occupancy octrees, signed distance fields, or mesh collision models) cannot run inside a 1 ms control interrupt merely because the MCU owns final actuation privilege. It therefore runs on the host as a separately scheduled, bounded job, and its result reaches the MCU as the trajectory record. The MCU’s own admission check then verifies that record against bounded, qualified local evidence (the resident stop suffix, its entry state, and the commitment deadline) without traversing the global map. The hard real-time cyclic path evaluates current joint state, remaining lease validity, and available actuator effort on every tick before selecting and dispatching the next physical command.

An in-line checker places its worst-case execution time directly inside the control-loop deadline, consuming a portion of the available compute tick. A parallel monitor instead operates asynchronously alongside the proposal pipeline, avoiding added forward latency during nominal execution but contributing detection latency and trip delay to the physical response budget. Either architectural placement must ensure privilege isolation. The untrusted proposer must never be able to bypass the selected output, and the system must preserve the dedicated sensing and actuation channels required by the fallback controller. As Two Paths and the Resources They Share will show from the resources each candidate placement shares, isolating these execution domains across memory spaces or heterogeneous processing cores is what allows the safety monitor and its fallback mechanisms to survive the very proposer faults that trigger an intervention. The physical stopping budget supplies the non-negotiable temporal and spatial boundaries that this hardware split must satisfy.

Systems Perspective 12.3: The monitor independence rule
Any valid safety claim must explicitly define which proposer failure modes its sensing, compute, and actuation pathways survive. Placing a monitor on a separate physical core or processor does not establish true independence if that monitor shares a power rail, a memory bus, a sensor link, or an unisolated clock domain with the untrusted proposer. Shared hardware resources require provably bounded interference or a qualified, survivable fallback state. Any environmental condition that the monitor cannot observe directly must remain an explicit, documented operating premise rather than an unstated assumption.

The running example assumes qualified drive, scanner, and arm safety functions and a rated lease/heartbeat watchdog; the release case must name the scoped evidence supporting those functions. Independence, however, is not certification. The runtime permission path is not the machine’s certified functional safety layer, and beneath it sit the certified safety functions embedded within motor drives, safety laser scanners, and the dedicated safety controller. These hardware-interlocked mechanisms carry the formal integrity claims that certification bodies audit against industrial standards. The primary operational duty of the software permission path is to regulate the learned proposer proactively so that the robot never trips those certified hardware interlocks during normal operation. Section 12.8.3 maps each dynamic response developed throughout this chapter onto that underlying certified safety floor once the complete response hierarchy is established, and names the one narrow MCU function that does carry a rating.

12.7.1 Where the thesis bends

When a learned locomotion or manipulation policy emits raw joint targets or motor torques at the control rate instead of handing the permission path a multi-step trajectory, the enforcer loses the ability to verify a collision-free stopping continuation before motion begins. It must then either run an active fallback model on every tick or watch its projections induce distributional shift and boundary chattering. Admission and the cyclic check in this chapter both assume that the proposer hands the permission path a plan it can judge before motion: kinematic intent lives in the learned policy, and deterministic motor drives close the torque loops. Much of contemporary robot learning practice abandons this classical division, outputting joint targets at the control rate above stiff proportional-derivative loops (Lee et al. 2020) or emitting motor torques end-to-end. The third law, proposal is not permission (principle 3), does not disappear in such direct control designs. Instead, the range of physical guarantees that the runtime permission path can uphold narrows in precise, quantifiable ways.

Lee, Joonho, Jemin Hwangbo, Lorenz Wellhausen, Vladlen Koltun, and Marco Hutter. 2020. “Learning Quadrupedal Locomotion over Challenging Terrain.” Science Robotics 5 (47): eabc5986.

Four guarantees survive because each acts directly on raw commands at the causal boundary, without requiring a forward plan. A per-tick limit on rate and amplitude bounds torque slew and joint velocity at the hardware register interface. The kinetic barrier of equation 6 operates directly in effort space, so a CBF-QP can filter the policy’s output without converting it to an intermediate kinematic setpoint. The passivity-based energy tank of section 12.6.2 monitors power exchange and bounds the physical work the policy can inject into the environment during contact. Beneath all of these, low-level geofencing, safely-limited speed monitoring, and torque removal backed by mechanical spring brakes remain permanently active under the software stack. Communication timing improves along one dimension. If a qualified freshness deadline is selected at one tick and an independent watchdog enforces it, policy silence is detected within that tick plus bus transport delay, which reduces the lease-expiration travel row of table 1 from 80.6 mm to 2.6 mm at the aisle speed.

What is lost is admission. Without an explicit multi-step trajectory, the system cannot verify the collision-free stopping suffix before the robot commits to motion. The enforcer must instead evaluate the machine’s dynamic stopping reservation on every tick against an active fallback controller that maintains its own internal braking model. On a wheeled mobile base, that fallback is straightforward to formulate. The controller executes a qualified service-braking response along the current heading, followed by wheel restraint after confirmed standstill. On an underactuated balancing machine, such as a bipedal humanoid, the fallback is itself an active balance controller; cutting motor torque produces an immediate uncontrolled fall rather than a safe state.

Runtime projection also alters the closed sensorimotor loop under which the policy was trained. Every time the enforcer projects a proposed action onto the admissible set, it delivers a state to the environment that the policy never intended, precipitating distributional shift in subsequent observations. This penalty surfaces directly in task performance, as quantified by the intervention metrics of section 12.6. Frequent overrides cause the machine to chatter erratically along safety boundaries or freeze conservatively short of its goal. To reconcile this tension, modern architectures adopt filter-aware policy training, incorporating the safety filter directly into the training objective so that the neural network learns to avoid the boundary rather than encountering the enforcer for the first time on physical hardware (Hsu et al. 2024). The core thesis still holds at the causal boundary, where untrusted proposals never bypass runtime enforcement. What bends is how much of the safety argument can be settled mathematically before motion begins vs. how much performance the machine must sacrifice to settle the remainder while moving.

12.8 The Fallback Ladder

A safety filter must select its fallback before the admissible control set becomes empty. Once the chosen filter has an empty feasible intersection, it cannot maintain its modeled invariant. A qualified alternate response may mitigate damage or support its own narrower containment claim, but selecting it does not restore the lost guarantee. Furthermore, physical systems cannot rely on a single, binary kill switch. Abruptly severing DC bus power whenever a control proposal approaches a barrier boundary induces uncontrolled deceleration, mechanical shock, and incessant false-positive mission aborts. Conversely, complex software optimization is futile during a hardware fault such as an inverter shoot-through, because the fault destroys gate-driver field-effect transistors (FETs) milliseconds before an interrupt handler can even dispatch.

To resolve these competing physical realities, the permission path maintains a deterministic ladder of resident protective responses. The runtime arbiter evaluates plant kinematics, remaining sensor validity, and hardware integrity on every control cycle, selecting deterministically among minimally invasive projection, closed-loop position holding, controlled deceleration, or complete torque removal. The name records an ordering by how much control authority each response retains, from a corrected proposal down to unpowered restraint, not a sequence to descend. The arbiter never climbs down the rungs one at a time; it jumps directly to the one whose prerequisites still hold.

Definition 12.3: Dynamic fallback ladder

Dynamic fallback ladder is the set of resident protective responses (\(\ControlInput \in \{\SafeSetProjection(\NominalControlCommand), \PoweredHoldCommand, \ControlledStopCommand, \TorqueCutoffCommand\}\)). A deterministic arbiter selects one of them on every tick according to the check that failed (an invariant violation, a timing overrun, or a hardware fault) and the measured state of the plant. One trigger can therefore select different rungs in motion and at standstill.

  1. Significance: Physical AI systems cannot treat all faults identically. Cutting actuator power on dynamic, underactuated, or high-momentum plants (for example, legged robots mid-stride or multirotors in flight) precipitates immediate catastrophic falls or tumbling. These platforms require state-matched active deceleration before executing terminal torque removal.
  2. Distinction: Unlike software exception handlers that unwind the call stack or terminate a user-space thread, a dynamic fallback ladder operates against physical momentum. The arbiter matches each rung to the remaining valid sensor channels, surviving control authority, and available stopping clearance.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the rungs as an escalation sequence where lower rungs, such as safe torque off (STO), inherently provide faster or safer arrest. Each rung possesses distinct entry prerequisites; invoking an unpowered torque inhibit on an airborne drone or an unbraked mobile base allows the machine to coast freely without arresting motion.

12.8.1 The four rungs of the fallback ladder

Walking down protective responses one step at a time like a software exception cascade fails on physical hardware: commanding a standstill position hold while the base is rolling at 1.3 m/s saturates the drives, while cutting drive torque before active braking completes lets the vehicle coast into the obstacle. The dynamic fallback architecture therefore organizes protection across four resident responses (PROJECT, HOLD, STOP, and INHIBIT). The deterministic arbiter samples measured plant state (velocity, position, payload loading) and fault severity on every millisecond tick to dispatch the response whose compute, sensing, and actuation prerequisites still hold:

  1. PROJECT, minimal-intervention projection in MCU firmware. The primary safety filter executes on every control cycle while the feasible control set is nonempty (\(\SafeControlSet \neq \emptyset\)), the proposal lease is fresh, and tracking error remains within bounds (\(\|\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant)\| \le \TrackingErrorBound\)). It computes the minimal-intervention torque \(\CertifiedControlAction = \arg\min_{\ControlInput} \|\ControlInput - \NominalControlCommand\|^2\) subject to active barrier half-spaces within its 150 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) cycle allocation. It makes the smallest feasible effort correction under the stated invariance assumptions; the planner must still account for any changed task-space path.
  2. HOLD, powered hold at standstill in MCU firmware. When the machine is stationary, the arbiter selects an energized position hold, analogous to an IEC 60204-1 Category 2 stop: safe stop 2 (SS2) followed by safe operating stop (SOS), the drive function that monitors standstill.15 Its protective validity stems from that certified standstill monitor rather than the position loop gain. Because IEC 60204-1 permits only Category 0 or Category 1 for an emergency stop, HOLD can never serve as an E-stop response (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016a). The drives maintain coordinate \(\PoweredHoldState\) under closed-loop position control, modulating inverter phase currents against gravity and static loads. While this rung incurs continuous Joule heating in motor windings, it eliminates friction brake wear and preserves kinematic calibration, enabling instantaneous task resumption when a valid trajectory proposal arrives. On the warehouse mobile manipulator, this rung handles lapsed leases or lost host heartbeats specifically when the platform is already at standstill.
  3. STOP, controlled dynamic stop in MCU firmware. When a machine in active transit loses proposal authority, tracking error diverges, or an obstacle breaches the stopping envelope, the response is a resident, state-matched controlled stop, analogous to an IEC 60204-1 Category 1 stop (safe stop 1, SS1).
