Its certified counterpart is the ramp-monitored variant (SS1-r), which checks deceleration against a bounded profile throughout the arrest; the unmonitored, time-controlled variant (SS1-t) merely removes torque after a fixed delay without verifying that the planned ramp actually decelerated the load (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016b). The resident jerk-limited S-curve stop suffix of section 12.2 serves this role provided the entry state falls within its validated domain. Executed autonomously by bare-metal MCU firmware directly from local SRAM, this stop peaks at the credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\), returning kinetic energy through motor inverters to the DC bus for absorption by the qualified regeneration path. Power removal and mechanical brake clamping occur only after confirmed zero velocity.
  4. INHIBIT, torque inhibit and spring-brake clamping by hardware interlocks. When the safety microcontroller suffers a brownout, clock loss, or watchdog timeout, all software decision logic ceases. Dedicated, out-of-band hardware interlocks trigger STO to de-energize gate drive power supplies and drop hold current to electromechanical spring-applied brakes. This terminal rung removes motor torque without software intervention; the mechanical brakes provide only their independently qualified holding or dynamic stopping capacity.

15 Machine Stop Categories: IEC 60204-1 distinguishes uncontrolled power removal (Category 0), controlled stopping followed by power removal (Category 1), and controlled stopping with power retained (Category 2). A category defines electrical power status rather than stopping distance; every rung therefore requires its own empirically validated stopping measurement.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016a. IEC 60204-1:2016: Safety of Machinery, Electrical Equipment of Machines, Part 1: General Requirements. International Electrotechnical Commission.

When a machine in motion loses proposal authority or violates a tracking bound, the arbiter engages a controlled dynamic stop, operating analogously to an IEC 60204-1 Category 1 stop. On the warehouse mobile manipulator base, this rung executes the resident stop suffix pre-admitted alongside the motion trajectory, rather than attempting to synthesize a stopping trajectory on the fly during a fault. In steady-state cruising with zero acceleration, this pre-computed profile matches the operating state at fault onset directly; nonzero acceleration states require their own qualified state-matched suffixes. From an aisle cruise velocity of 1.3 m/s, the jerk-limited S-curve suffix sustains a credible peak deceleration of 2 m/s² between its jerk transitions. The entire maneuver spans 931.25 ms and traverses 605.31 mm, the stop-suffix travel the stopping budget of section 12.2 reserves at this speed. Because the deceleration phase channels kinetic energy back through the inverter into the DC bus, bus capacitance and braking choppers must absorb this regenerative surge without triggering a bus overvoltage fault. Complete electrical power removal, if mandated by protocol, occurs only after sensors verify standstill.

Beyond firmware-driven stops, a separate out-of-band circuit can inhibit motor torque generation entirely. Drive STO cuts gate-drive bias to isolate inverter FETs, but it does not disconnect the DC bus, energize mechanical friction brakes, or actively decelerate a moving rotor; an unbraked motor will simply coast under its existing momentum. In robust architectures, an external safety controller simultaneously drives a separately rated contactor and spring-applied mechanical brakes. Engineers must evaluate the dynamic stopping distance of this combined pathway as an integrated system, because dropping drive torque while an arm is under gravitational load or while a vehicle is at speed can exacerbate the hazard. For the warehouse mobile manipulator, this pathway, STO combined with nine spring brakes that engage between 30 ms and 80 ms after coil flux decay, constitutes the unbudgeted INHIBIT rung. It serves exclusively as a hardware backstop for MCU cycle failure, and the primary stopping envelope claims do not rely on it.

An undervoltage fault on the shared drive bus can invoke the STOP rung only while inverter gate drives, angular encoders, and control logic retain adequate voltage for closed-loop deceleration. The isolated permission rail maintains power to logic circuits, transceivers, and brake coils for 2 s, but it does not supply the motor currents required for braking torque. Consequently, timing guarantees for powered stopping assume a surviving drive bus. Under this condition, dynamic braking begins within 22 ms of the fault flag; the hold-up hypothetical uses the pre-dispatch inspected-floor ceiling of 1.23 m/s, rather than an admitted operating configuration, and its stop requires 1211.4 ms. Mechanical brake engagement at standstill adds at most 80 ms. This entire sequence completes within 1313.43 ms, preserving a 686.57 ms buffer of control logic hold-up.

↩︎ The isolated permission rail and its capacitive hold-up are established in Cross-budget coupling and permission rail isolation.

This analysis confirms the control-supply duration budget, whereas the electrical drive-energy budget requires separate qualification. Systems engineers must prove through empirical testing that the DC bus capacitance, inverter power stages, and regenerative dump resistors sustain controlled deceleration under worst-case electrical fault conditions. If the drive stage cannot guarantee this power integrity, the system must either adopt an unpowered dynamic braking configuration or restrict its operational safety envelope; INHIBIT cannot be credited with standard Category 1 stopping performance. Similarly, at standstill, an undervoltage fault must trigger verified mechanical brake engagement before the logic supply decays, rather than attempting to maintain an active HOLD indefinitely on finite capacitance.

Physical AI systems engineers must distinguish between fail-safe and fail-operational plants when configuring terminal fallbacks. In a machine already at a mechanically restrained standstill, removing drive torque is safe once mechanical engagement is verified. On underactuated, open-loop unstable, or high-momentum platforms, however, abruptly removing actuator torque directly initiates a catastrophic failure mode:

  1. Aerial platforms (multirotors and eVTOLs): Cutting motor torque drops aerodynamic thrust to zero instantly, transforming an airborne vehicle into an uncontrolled ballistic projectile.
  2. Dynamic legged systems (bipedal humanoids): During dynamic locomotion, the robot’s center of mass frequently falls outside the support polygon; stability depends on active ground-reaction forces and momentum exchange. Suddenly removing joint torques causes an unrecoverable fall, with impact severity governed by gait phase and available compliance.
  3. High-speed autonomous vehicles: De-energizing steering actuators or electric traction motors at highway speeds strips away the control authority required to execute a coordinated deceleration, leaving the vehicle at the mercy of passive road friction.

The physical channel that dispatches a hardware torque-removal request is the out-of-band interlock that Actuation Authority traced (Illustrative out-of-band interlock and STO path). In it, dual linked emergency-stop contacts, two force-guided relays whose external device monitoring (EDM) contacts block a restart after a weld, and two STO inputs on the drive leave no single fault able to defeat the trip. What the INHIBIT rung needs from that circuit is its third stage, drawn again here with the brake in its own loop (figure 9), because removing drive torque (\(\CommandedJointTorque = 0\)) and arresting physical momentum are distinct functions that need separate validation:

  1. Torque removal (Stage 3, drive side): De-energizing the safety relays drops the drive’s STO inputs, which de-energize the gate drive power supply for the three-phase inverter bridge, rendering the power MOSFETs or insulated-gate bipolar transistors (IGBTs) high-impedance, so the motor can no longer produce electromagnetic torque. STO alone does not apply mechanical braking. An unbraked motor carries momentum and may coast freely.
  2. Mechanical restraint (Stage 3, brake side): A separate coil circuit powers the spring-applied electromechanical friction brake. When power is removed, coil magnetic flux decays, allowing internal springs to physically clamp friction pads against the motor shaft, applying the brake without electrical power. A holding-brake rating does not establish the ability to arrest a moving load; dynamic stopping requires separate energy, torque, and response qualification.

That separation between software control and hardwired, out-of-band trip circuits is not a stylistic preference. When the processor running the control loop stalls or corrupts its own state, only a physically independent path can still remove drive enable and clamp the brakes. The precedent that the lineage table of The Four Bedrock Laws attaches to the third law shows the cost of removing that hardware path and trusting software logic to gate a high-energy actuator on its own (war story 12.1).

War Story 12.1: Therac-25 software interlock elimination
Context: Between 1985 and 1987, the Therac-25 medical linear accelerator administered massive radiation overdoses to six cancer patients after system designers eliminated the electromechanical interlocks used in previous models, assigning safety-critical beam containment entirely to software (Leveson and Turner 1993).

Mechanism: Software race conditions and shared memory corruption in the operator interface allowed the machine to fire its high-current electron beam (25 MeV) without rotating the tungsten target and beam-flattening filter into position. Because the physical microswitches that previously interlocked beam activation had been removed, the underlying hardware possessed no independent means to detect that the operational mode conflicted with the turntable state.

Impact: Six patients received radiation doses estimated between 4,000 and 20,000 rads (up to 100 times the therapeutic level), causing severe tissue necrosis and three direct fatalities (Leveson and Turner 1993).

Response: Investigators mandated the restoration of independent hardware interlocks, requiring positive electromechanical verification of turntable configuration prior to beam generation, alongside formal software safety lifecycle standards.

Systems lesson: A software-based protection layer cannot serve as its own independent backstop. A runtime barrier filter may exercise binding authority over nominal control proposals, but the architecture still requires an out-of-band, physically independent trip mechanism to survive processor lockups, memory corruption, and firmware bugs. Moreover, because torque removal alone does not guarantee deceleration, the surviving physical response must match the plant’s dynamic envelope.

The four rungs run on two paths (figure 10). PROJECT, HOLD, and STOP run in safety MCU firmware at the 1 kHz tick and reach the drives through one arbiter, which selects a single actuation command \(\SelectedActuationCommand\); INHIBIT bypasses the firmware on a hardware path that removes gate-drive supply (STO) and drops spring-brake holding current when the processor itself fails. The rungs trade response latency, control authority, and recovery cost, and the arbiter selects directly among those whose entry prerequisites still hold. A controlled stop demands verified drive electronics and sufficient stopping clearance; losing drive integrity forces unpowered mitigation with strictly weaker physical guarantees. Selection is also only as sound as the state evidence behind it. The 2023 Cruise pullover, which When the Responsibilities Fail Together set beside Tempe as a failure after impact, selected a motion fallback on the strength of a missing track (war story 12.2).

War Story 12.2: Cruise post-impact fallback (2023)
Context: In October 2023, an autonomous Cruise robotaxi in San Francisco encountered a pedestrian who had been struck by an adjacent human-driven vehicle and propelled into the robotaxi’s travel lane (Exponent, Inc. 2024).

Mechanism: The autonomous vehicle initiated an immediate emergency stop upon detecting the impact. However, the collision-classification subsystem erroneously localized the pedestrian to the side of the vehicle rather than under the chassis, categorizing the event as a side collision. The operational design domain for side collisions specified a secondary pullover maneuver to clear traffic. Because the perception pipeline had tracked the pedestrian only intermittently before the collision, the tracker marked the lane ahead as clear, prompting the vehicle to accelerate from standstill into the pullover (Exponent, Inc. 2024).

Impact: Executing the pullover dragged the pedestrian, who remained pinned beneath the vehicle’s floorpan, approximately 20 ft (6.1 m) at speeds reaching 7.7 mph (3.4 m/s), causing severe trauma (Quinn Emanuel Urquhart and Sullivan, LLP 2024). The California DMV subsequently suspended Cruise’s autonomous testing and deployment permits, prompting Cruise to halt fleet operations nationwide and recall software across 950 vehicles (California Department of Motor Vehicles 2023; Exponent, Inc. 2024).

Response: A post-collision fallback must prohibit renewed motion whenever state uncertainty persists. In this scenario, retaining a locked standstill and mandating physical inspection prevents the system from treating missing perceptual tracks as free space. Teleoperation feeds cannot substitute for verified local sensor coverage.

Systems lesson: An embodied fallback cannot infer free space from the absence of sensor tracks. Every transition across fallback states requires positive evidence verifying that assumed physical invariants hold, paired with failsafe physical restraints while state ambiguity remains unresolved.

Exponent, Inc. 2024. Cruise LLC–October 2, 2023, Incident Report. Exponent, Inc.
California Department of Motor Vehicles. 2023. DMV Statement on Cruise LLC Suspension.
Quinn Emanuel Urquhart and Sullivan, LLP. 2024. Report to the Boards of Directors of Cruise LLC, GM Cruise Holdings LLC, and General Motors Holdings LLC Regarding the October 2, 2023 Accident in San Francisco. Quinn Emanuel Urquhart; Sullivan, LLP.

12.8.2 Industrial stop categories and the safe torque off trap

On a moving mobile base or a gravity-loaded arm, Safe Torque Off (STO) is not an active brake: removing drive torque immediately leaves the base coasting on wheel momentum and lets the arm drop during the milliseconds before its mechanical friction brakes clamp. Decelerating the mobile manipulator safely instead uses closed-loop motor torque during the active braking phase and engages mechanical holding brakes only once the plant reaches confirmed standstill. Industrial standards distinguish machine-level stop categories from drive-level safety functions across this sequence. IEC 60204-1 classifies whether electrical power remains connected during and after deceleration. Functional safety standards such as IEC 61800-5-2 define the drive functions, including STO, SS1, and SS2, that execute individual stages of a response, and the HOLD and STOP rungs borrowed their names from them. Table 3 maps these formal classifications to their concrete physical obligations.

12.8.3 Certified safety functions beneath the permission path

Citing an unrated numerical QP solver or software evidence-age check as the primary hazard control in a safety case fails certification audit immediately, because complex solver code without formal tool qualification and hardware diagnostic coverage cannot carry a safety integrity claim. In an embodied deployment, not all firmware executing the fallback ladder carries formal functional safety certification, so a certification engineer partitions software claims by the integrity of the underlying hardware substrate. On a production machine, formal guarantees rest primarily on a second, lower layer: dedicated safety functions in the motor drives, the optical safety scanner, and the arm’s safety controller, each architected and validated to a Performance Level (PL) under ISO 13849-1 (International Organization for Standardization 2023a). A Performance Level grades a safety function rather than a component, by the architecture category of its channels, their mean time to dangerous failure, their diagnostic coverage, and their defenses against common-cause failure (The Independence Requirement and the Two-Processor Hypothesis); IEC 61508 grades the same functions by Safety Integrity Level (SIL) (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010). These hardened functions substantiate the certified protective envelope. Above them, the MCU permission path operates primarily as an unrated performance layer designed to keep the learned policy inside the admissible set, preventing unnecessary trips of the hard safety floor. Only one narrow component of this MCU layer carries a safety rating, the watchdog that revokes authority when the chunk lease expires or the host heartbeat falls silent.

International Organization for Standardization. 2023b. ISO 3691-4:2023: Industrial Trucks, Safety Requirements and Verification, Part 4: Driverless Industrial Trucks and Their Systems. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2025a. ISO 10218-1:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 1: Industrial Robots. International Organization for Standardization.

Table 4 maps each response and speed channel onto its certified industrial counterpart. The core drive functions derive from IEC 61800-5-2 (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016b): safe torque off (STO), safe stop 1 (SS1), safe stop 2 (SS2), safe operating stop (SOS) (which monitors standstill), safely limited speed (SLS), and safe brake control (SBC) (which drives the mechanical brake coil through a monitored output). For the mobile base, ISO 3691-4 assigns required performance level (PLr) d to braking system control and personnel detection within the travel path, while assigning PLr b to the electromechanical parking brake (International Organization for Standardization 2023b; TÜV Rheinland 2021). For robotic manipulators, ISO 10218-1:2025 classifies systems into Class I (manipulator mass under 10 kg with strictly limited force and velocity) and Class II, mandating PL d (or SIL 2) across all safety functions on Class II arms (International Organization for Standardization 2025a; Hartmann et al. 2026). Because the warehouse arm weighs 18 kg, it is governed by Class II mandates.

As the first row of table 4 indicates, the online CBF-QP, feasibility tests, and sensor evidence-age checks operate without formal safety ratings. Consequently, safety cases cannot cite these complex numerical filters as certified protective measures; instead, the enforcement record treats their correct execution as an operational premise. System architects face two choices when structuring this boundary. An engineering team can treat all MCU-level admission checks strictly as an unrated performance layer, relying entirely on the certified hardware floor to prevent harm. Alternatively, the team can certify the entire MCU firmware stack to functional safety standards (such as IEC 61508 or ISO 26262), subjecting matrix solvers and barrier algorithms to exhaustive formal methods, tool qualification, and deterministic execution bounds.

The running machine assumes the hybrid path. Its qualified watchdog, the table’s second row, independently commands STO or SS1 at the drive level when the chunk lease expires or the host heartbeat falls silent. Its bounded scope reduces the qualification burden, while isolation, failure behavior, timing, and lifecycle evidence remain necessary. Meanwhile, the complex admission checks remain unrated, leaving the certified optical scanner and drive-integrated safety functions to handle primary hazard mitigation. This split architecture demands strict physical and temporal isolation between the rated watchdog and unrated solver routines to eliminate common-cause faults.

↪ How the release case treats the certified watchdog as a rated safety function and the unrated admission checks as empirical premises is developed in The standards map for the running machine.

International Organization for Standardization. 2025b. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2016. ISO/TS 15066:2016: Robots and Robotic Devices, Collaborative Robots. International Organization for Standardization.
Hartmann, Daniel, Kristýna Hamříková, Aleš Vysocký, Vendula Laciok, and Aleš Bernatík. 2026. “Evolution of Safety Requirements in Industrial Robotics: Comparative Analysis of ISO 10218-1/2 (2011 Vs. 2025) and Integration of ISO/TS 15066.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2602.17822.
Marvel, Jeremy A., and Rick Norcross. 2017. “Implementing Speed and Separation Monitoring in Collaborative Robot Workcells.” Robotics and Computer-Integrated Manufacturing 44: 144–55. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rcim.2016.08.001.

The robotic arm introduces collaborative operational modes beyond those required by the mobile base. Speed and separation monitoring (SSM) continuously modulates or halts arm velocity to maintain a protective clearance envelope around human workers, while power and force limiting (PFL) caps transient and quasi-static contact forces during intentional physical collaboration. Standard ISO 10218-2:2025 incorporates the collaborative requirements previously defined in ISO/TS 15066 (International Organization for Standardization 2025b, 2016). The 2025 standard formalizes SSM within section 5.14.5 and normative Annex L, and specifies PFL limits across anatomical regions in section 5.14.6 and informative Annex M (Hartmann et al. 2026). The protective separation distance is governed by: \[\ProtectiveSeparationDistance = \PersonApproachTravel + \RobotReactionTravel + \RobotStoppingTravel + \IntrusionDistance + \PersonPositionUncertainty + \RobotPositionUncertainty,\] where \(\PersonApproachTravel = \HumanWalkingSpeedBound (\RobotReactionTime + \StoppingDuration)\) represents worker displacement during robot reaction latency \(\RobotReactionTime\) and stopping duration \(\StoppingDuration\). Next, \(\RobotReactionTravel = \RobotApproachSpeed \RobotReactionTime\) is robot displacement prior to brake onset, and \(\RobotStoppingTravel\) is the physical braking distance. The term \(\IntrusionDistance\) is the penetration distance permitted by sensor resolution. Finally, \(\PersonPositionUncertainty\) and \(\RobotPositionUncertainty\) denote positional uncertainties for the human and robot, respectively (Marvel and Norcross 2017).

Evaluating this expression for the descending arm highlights the relative weight of human vs. robot motion. The ISO 13855 human approach speed is \(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound =\) 1.6 m/s (International Organization for Standardization 2024). The vision-path latency is \(\RobotReactionTime =\) 133.6 ms, and the tool-center-point velocity is \(\RobotApproachSpeed =\) 0.96 m/s. The gravity-biased joint deceleration from section 12.3 completes in \(\StoppingDuration =\) 166 ms across \(\RobotStoppingTravel =\) 79 mm. With these values, the human displacement term is \(\PersonApproachTravel =\) 479 mm and the robot reaction displacement is \(\RobotReactionTravel =\) 128 mm. Together, these preliminary dynamic components total 686 mm before accounting for sensor intrusion margin \(\IntrusionDistance\) and spatial uncertainties \(\PersonPositionUncertainty\) and \(\RobotPositionUncertainty\).

International Organization for Standardization. 2024. ISO 13855:2024: Safety of Machinery, Positioning of Safeguards with Respect to the Approach of the Human Body. International Organization for Standardization.

Human movement accounts for 70 percent of this cumulative margin. Just as in the walking-person stopping budget of section 12.2.1, human velocity during the combined reaction and deceleration phases dominates the required clearance. Against an approaching human, total stopping duration imposes a far heavier spatial tax than the robot’s physical braking distance alone. Because standard vision pipelines lack functional safety ratings, a certified SSM implementation must use dedicated, safety-qualified sensors to measure separation and bound \(\RobotReactionTime\).

The standards distinguish how they treat obstacle velocity. Standard ISO 3691-4 specifies detection assuming a stationary person standing in the travel path (TÜV Rheinland 2021); consequently, vehicle testing protocols evaluate protective envelopes against the static-obstacle formulation of equation 1. If a worker continues walking toward the vehicle throughout the braking sequence, the relative closing velocity exceeds the standard’s baseline assumptions. In contrast, SSM explicitly models dynamic human approach velocity (\(\HumanWalkingSpeedBound\)), explaining why mobile base navigation requires an explicit aisle-crossing protocol whereas robotic manipulation requires an active separation or contact-limiting channel. For collaborative handoffs, the enforcement record adopts the standardized biomechanical model of the human hand, distinguishing between unconstrained (transient) and clamped (quasi-static) contact scenarios. While simplified models often approximate PFL as a single lumped elastic collision with a fixed force threshold, ISO 10218-2 specifies distinct permissible force and pressure ceilings across individual anatomical regions. A complete architectural map correlating each subsystem, governing regulatory standard, and validation artifact is synthesized in the release decision framework (Standards map for the warehouse mobile manipulator (as of 2026)).

TÜV Rheinland. 2021. ISO 3691-4:2020: A Standard for Automated Guided Vehicles. White paper.
Checkpoint 12.3: Choosing the physical fallback

Before selecting a defensive fallback strategy, review the requirements governing state-dependent stopping modes, safety functions, and timing reserves:

12.9 Runtime Enforcement Failure Modes

Every guarantee in this chapter has rested on premises about the computer that runs the filter and the plant it models: a held-up logic rail, a bounded cycle, a credible deceleration, and a bus that delivers the admitted command on time. On the warehouse mobile manipulator each of those premises already carries a concrete spatial price. A missed tick costs 1.3 mm of travel at the aisle speed. A lease honored by the host instead of the MCU turns a 200 ms planning stall into 182 mm of unbraked travel and a 50.99 mm overrun. An unprotected integrator spends 195 mm against a 131.0 mm spare. Gravity on the arm and mug takes 75 percent of the joint’s peak torque and quadruples the descending stop. Runtime enforcement failures trace back to four classes, one for each premise:

  1. Common-cause infrastructure failures: When the learned policy and the safety filter share power rails, clock sources, reset lines, or thermal paths, one fault can disable both. A current surge during hard acceleration can sag a shared supply until it resets both the neural accelerator and the microcontroller that monitors it, and a shared reset or clock fault can silence the monitor at the instant the policy issues an errant command. The running machine therefore holds its permission logic on an isolated, held-up rail (section 12.8.1).
  2. Timing overruns and execution jitter: A quadratic program that stalls on an ill-conditioned active set, or a filter scheduled on a non-deterministic operating system, spends braking clearance while it computes. An overrunning routine cannot be trusted to detect its own stall, which is why the declared 250 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) cycle comes with a qualified preempting timer or independent mechanism that selects the resident fallback when a deadline expires (section 12.6.4).
  3. Plant mismatch and unobservable state drift: The filter evaluates safety against a model. A floor film that lowers friction, a payload that shifts the center of mass, or winding heat that lowers peak torque removes control authority the model still credits, so the solver can admit a deceleration the hardware cannot deliver. Where onboard sensors cannot observe the shift, the safe set must carry qualified uncertainty margins or the operating domain must restrict speed, as the complete optimistic inspected-floor screen falls to 1.17 m/s, before driven-wheel load share and available braking are qualified (section 12.2).
  4. Interconnect contention and transport delays: State reads and command writes must cross physical buses. Unprioritized telemetry or logging can queue a joint reading until it is stale, or hold a filtered command in a transmit queue while the drive keeps executing the previous high-speed setpoint. The running machine carries its admitted command in a cyclic EtherCAT frame (stage 5 of the cycle budget) and charges the board link to the dispatch-to-admission budget.

None of the four yields to a better solver or an automotive-grade microcontroller. Each is answered by evidence. Common causes and contention need isolation and bounded interference, which Workload Placement tests on silicon. Overruns need a verified execution bound with an independent fallback. Plant mismatch needs worst-case rather than nominal parameter margins. All four need a record of every enforcement decision, with its solver latency, active constraints, and sensor timestamps, so that an incident can be traced to the policy, the plant, or the infrastructure.

12.10 The Enforcement Record

When a warehouse mobile manipulator halts at a rack end because runtime safety logic rejected a motion proposal, forensic analysis requires unambiguous provenance: which threshold failed, which physical detector measured the violation, and which fallback rung took control of the actuators. The enforcement record is the tenth record in the record chain, the point where offline characterization meets online execution. It formalizes runtime safety across three cooperating artifacts:

  1. Enforcement contract: The signed, immutable specification compiled into microcontroller firmware that fixes qualified tracking bounds \(\TrackingErrorBound\), barrier coefficients, timer deadlines, delivered-effort error and response-delay bounds, and fallback routing tables before deployment.
  2. Admitted command: The record’s per-tick output. On each millisecond control cycle, the microcontroller gatekeeper generates an admitted command for transmission across the causal boundary. This payload contains the admitted or projected control \(\CertifiedControlAction\), the verified barrier margin \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\), tracking error validation \(\TrackingErrorDistance(\TimeInstant) \le \TrackingErrorBound\), and the remaining hardware lease. The gatekeeper forwards only the bare setpoint and protective envelope to the motor drives, committing the complete supervisory state to the audit log.
  3. Enforcement audit log: The high-rate forensic stream that captures per-tick proposal evaluations, intervention vectors \(\FiniteChange \ControlInput = \CertifiedControlAction - \NominalControlCommand\), quadratic programming (QP) solve latencies, and veto events in non-volatile storage.

Together, these artifacts render safety decisions fully auditable without burdening the actuator bus. The contract predefines the admissible operating envelope, the admitted command tags the immediate actuator setpoint, and the audit log preserves the causal chain of evidence. Real-time enforcement depends entirely on the protected command path, deterministic loop timing, and verified physical braking capabilities.

12.10.1 The admitted command schema

If the gatekeeper emits a bare torque vector without a monotonic sequence counter, a digest of the admitted parent trajectory, or the remaining lease duration, a replayed frame or a stalled microcontroller cannot be distinguished from a live command, and a post-incident audit cannot reconstruct which safety margins were checked on that millisecond tick. At each control cycle, the gatekeeper therefore produces an admitted command that establishes runtime authority at the drive interface (table 5). Its fields explicitly bind the actuator setpoint to its parent trajectory, verified safety margins, and active fallback state. Because each entry records a decision made under assumed physical conditions, runtime containment still relies on those assumptions matching physical reality.

Constructing the enforcement record synthesizes four upstream records across the offline and runtime evidence strands, where the offline strand establishes physical plant capabilities and empirical policy competence while the runtime strand tracks live execution state. From the runtime strand, the trajectory record of The Trajectory Record supplies the admitted reference and its precomputed, dynamically feasible stop suffix. From the offline strand, the evaluation record of The Evaluation Record provides runtime monitor specifications and the empirical coverage-gap catalog that define the active supervisory channels. The policy manifest of The Policy Manifest defines the declared ODD so that the enforcer’s validity region is restricted to well-sampled operating regimes. The limit records of Measuring a Machine's Own Limits establish empirical braking rates, actuator onset delays, and torque envelopes alongside their operating preconditions. To these upstream parameters, the enforcement record contributes the formal guarantees of the gatekeeping pipeline. These are the qualified tracking bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) and the stopping metrics (\(\StoppingDuration, \StoppingDistance\), which bound the duration and displacement required to arrest plant momentum). They also include the control barrier function coefficients, the discrete execution period, the per-check thresholds mapped to fallback-ladder rungs, and the dynamic validity boundary.

The parent_trajectory_digest binds each admitted command to its originating trajectory and active chunk lease, providing forensic traceability back to the planner’s proposal. Digests bind content for integrity checks; sequence counters and monotonic freshness checks reject stale or replayed commands within the protected drive interface. Sender authenticity additionally requires an authenticated channel or message authentication. Similarly, the lease_remaining_ns field conveys remaining execution authority, but this parameter guarantees safety only when the drive firmware actively enforces lease expiration by triggering a deterministic physical fallback. Streaming these fields to the audit log, alongside nominal proposals, intervention deltas, barrier margins, tracking errors, and solver latencies, gives engineers the causal evidence needed to reconstruct anomalous behaviors within the temporal resolution of the sensor pipeline.

This division of responsibilities dictates an architectural split at the fieldbus boundary. Motor drives have no operational need for trajectory digests or barrier margins; they require immediate setpoints, current limits, and active fallback commands delivered with fail-safe transport guarantees. Industrial systems handle this through established safety bus architectures. For example, Safety over EtherCAT (FSoE) implements the black-channel principle, treating the underlying communication bus as inherently untrusted (International Electrotechnical Commission 2021). FSoE encapsulates drive commands within a safety container featuring dedicated cyclic redundancy checks (CRCs), unique connection identifiers, strict sequence counters, and independent watchdog timers. These measures detect communication faults; sender authentication against malicious traffic requires a separate security mechanism. When a communication watchdog expires, the drive autonomously engages its safe stop state without waiting for microcontroller intervention. The rich diagnostic context (digests, barrier margins, intervention vectors, and solver runtimes) streams to the audit log instead. An ad hoc safety-communication scheme for drive control must independently justify the fault detection and response properties covered by established functional-safety standards.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2021. IEC 61784-3:2021: Industrial Communication Networks, Profiles, Part 3: Functional Safety Fieldbuses, General Rules and Profile Definitions. International Electrotechnical Commission.

↪ The authority record’s per-tick entries, which log the ladder rung of any active fallback, are developed in Per-tick evidence tuples and the fault injection suite.

Filled in for the warehouse mobile manipulator, the enforcement record fixes the base’s stopping parameters at the rack end alongside the arm’s validity region and monitoring channels at the doorway and packing station. The tracking bound \(\TrackingErrorBound\) is 40 mm (matching row 7 of table 1), and the resident stopping trajectory is the jerk-limited S-curve suffix, which takes 931.25 ms and covers 605.31 mm from the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, or 1,031.25 ms and 773.44 mm from the 1.5 m/s drive limit. These spatial bounds are evaluated on a 1 ms loop period with a declared WCET of 250 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) inside a 400 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) deadline, supervised by three parallel timers: the 60 ms chunk lease, the 100 \(\text{Hz}\) host heartbeat with its 30 ms silence timeout, and the 5 ms hardware watchdog.

For the manipulator, the validity region governs both the doorway and the packing station. The baseline door policy matches the nominal target ODD of the evaluation record (the nominal plate, standard door resistance, a stationary base, and the guarded approach, whereas the recovery update analyzed in Qualifying a recovery update qualifies the displaced-plate cell under its own distinct record rather than expanding the baseline region). The packing station admits picking a mug from the conveyor under a live intent lease followed by coworker handover. Across these zones, manipulator monitoring channels enforce dynamic velocity ceilings at the TCP. Inside the door zone, the approach-speed channel from the evaluation record’s runtime_monitor_specs caps tool velocity at 0.03 m/s, whereas in unconstrained space the conveyor mug pick operates under the nominal 1 m/s free-space TCP limit. While a coworker holds accept_item (Human Authority, Reaction Time, and Clearance), a handover channel engages before the separating distance closes to the arm’s biased stop plus its blind-travel displacement and caps the approach toward the coworker’s hand at 0.10 m/s, well below the 0.24 m/s contact-force ceiling for a free hand capable of recoil. Because a hand trapped against a rack upright or structural link permits an impact speed of at most 0.053 m/s, this handover cap is valid only when physical workstation geometry provably precludes clamping (section 12.6.2), a condition that the release argument must substantiate.

For the mobile base, operating assumptions distinguish between nominal and low-friction surfaces, each carrying its own cryptographic digest in the release manifest. The standard configuration requires a qualified directional braking lower bound encompassing driven-wheel load share, traction, torque, and stability, where a friction coefficient of at least 0.204 serves only as a necessary optimistic all-weight screen rather than sufficient evidence that the design deceleration holds. By contrast, a restricted configuration accommodates degraded traction down to an inspected-floor friction of 0.12. The restricted illustration stipulates that its 1.177 m/s² braking rate is available (since inspection alone does not establish that lower bound), and its deceleration suffix uses that stipulation to stop in 1015.3 ms across 507.6 mm from 1 m/s beneath the 1.17 m/s complete inspected-floor screen, whose braking premise still requires qualification.

Each base entry incorporates the 63.3 ms dispatch-to-admission allocation and its own scenario-specific remaining clearance. The 48.7 mm stopping reserve belongs to the dry static-obstacle aisle candidate; the restricted configuration must recompute its reserve from its own speed and braking rate. Because this allocation represents an analytical budget, neither configuration is certified for admission until physical deceleration measurements under maximum payload confirm the modeled margin.

Each numerical entry in the record carries the evidence category of its limit record: measured, derived, datasheet, or illustrative. These categories reflect fundamentally different degrees of operational confidence. The base’s 931.25 ms stopping time, for instance, is a derived value. It follows mathematically from the geometry of the S-curve suffix under a stipulated jerk bound and an illustrative deceleration figure, assuming that motor drives faithfully deliver commanded current and tires maintain continuous traction. Only loaded stopping trials on the actual operating surface establish a measured value. Physical tests reveal empirical non-idealities that escape closed-form derivations, including electromechanical brake onset scatter, structural compliance, and surface contamination. Even then, empirical data supports a formal bound only when combined with worst-case fault models and defined operating limits. Explicitly recording the evidence category prevents an integrator from mistaking a derived stopping estimate for a verified hardware limit.

The record also carries the routing of section 12.8 as part of its contract. An arriving proposal can neither renew an active command nor revoke its authority merely by being transmitted, and each lease or heartbeat expiry, cycle loss, or bus undervoltage selects the rung that matches the machine’s state and the hardware that survives. Every field of the record is either an integrity token or an operational threshold evaluated against live execution state. These checks examine the proposal’s sequence counter and checksum, its stored dispatch age against 51.8 ms, reconstructed perception latency against validity and stopping-margin criteria, active elapsed time against the chunk lease, tracking error against the allowable envelope \(\TrackingErrorBound\), joint-velocity and torque-slew ceilings, barrier margins, phase-current and bus-voltage limits, and hardware watchdog timeouts. The contract binds each threshold to the independent sensing hardware that measures it and to the specific fallback rung selected upon violation. Table 6 organizes these rungs by residual system authority and specifies the hardware timer that triggers each transition.

↪ Byte layout: The enforcement record’s fields, types, and units are given in Enforcement record.

Because runtime safety guarantees require that plant dynamics remain inside the modeled operational envelope, the enforcement record bounds execution within an explicit validity region. The manipulator’s doorway speed ceiling illustrates how empirical evidence defines these bounds. The policy’s proposed 0.10 m/s approach, refused in section 12.5 on evidential and physical grounds, passes only the narrowest timing test, since its 2.5 ms unbraked compression time from first contact to the latch force limit exceeds the 2 ms contact-loop cycle. That naive unbraked deadline ignores both the force already accumulated before the tripwire fires and the compression distance the arm still needs to decelerate to rest, so the record keeps both grounds for refusal beside that pass, the post-trip stop that section 12.6.2 sized and the absence of trials at that speed, ensuring that no reader mistakes the timing pass for admission. The enforcer therefore clamps the command to the 0.03 m/s evidence-based ceiling, evaluates full contact dynamics, and records the active constraint in the audit log. In contrast, the coworker handover ceiling is derived from the structural impact model (equation 13), which caps approach velocity below the contact-force limit where modeled impact forces meet allowable biomechanical thresholds for an unconstrained hand. Faster proposals are projected onto this boundary and logged as intervention vectors.

When onboard estimators detect a state outside the validity envelope, such as an undeclared payload mass, the supervisory monitor flags the violation, revokes policy authority, and commands a state-matched stop. Environmental conditions that lack direct onboard sensing, however, cannot trigger dynamic runtime vetoes. Surface friction at the wheel-floor interface represents an unobservable state, because mobile bases carry no transducers to measure instantaneous traction coefficients beneath their wheels. Consequently, the standard configuration relies on verified floor friction as an operating precondition, while low-friction operations require manual floor inspection and commissioning of the restricted configuration.

↪ The floor-friction premise is enforced as an entry of the residual-claims register in A residual-claims register.

An enforcement record connects proposed neural trajectories to validated physical invariants, operational thresholds, and deterministic fallback responses. Algorithmic correctness, however, cannot compensate for an execution platform that violates its timing or braking commitments under load. An enforcer whose control barrier functions are mathematically verified will still fail if the couplings it still shares with the host, the board-link DMA into its memory, a supply rail, or a clock, delay its cycle under load during high-speed transit. In physical artificial intelligence, safety failures manifest in the time domain just as decisively as in algorithmic logic.

12.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A physical safety filter prevents collisions only as long as its control loop meets hard real-time execution deadlines and its state feedback accurately reflects reality. Safety qualification cannot rely solely on evaluating nominal control barrier equations under idealized laboratory conditions; real systems fail when host processors lock up, state estimators drift, or perception pipelines misclassify obstacles. Production testing must therefore exercise system response precisely when these core execution and sensing assumptions break down.

Pitfall: Assuming high-level policy computers can be relied upon to execute graceful shutdown.

A software exception handler on an application processor cannot arrest physical momentum when the operating system kernel panics. Consider a warehouse mobile manipulator whose high-level application processor experiences an operating system kernel panic while navigating an aisle at 1.3 m/s. Once the host kernel crashes, it can no longer generate deceleration trajectories or dispatch motor setpoints. Cutting the drive enable line to zero torque is equally hazardous, leaving the unbraked base to coast under momentum directly into the aisle racks. Relying on an operating system shutdown handler or a user-space exception hook assumes that the host software stack remains functional, an assumption that fails during kernel panics, hardware lockups, or memory exhaustion. Physical fail-safety requires stopping clearance and torque authority that persist independently of the host. In a robust architecture, the downstream MCU detects the missing heartbeat within its 30 ms timeout, comfortably within the stopping budget’s reserved lease margin. The MCU then immediately executes its resident jerk-limited stop suffix, decelerating the base to a complete stop in 931.25 ms across 605.31 mm. While host shutdown routines manage orderly transitions during routine operation, true fault tolerance demands that stopping authority resides in hardware and low-level firmware that survive host death.

Architect’s countermeasure: Decouple physical stopping authority from host processor lifecycles with two MCU timers that the host cannot service on the MCU’s behalf. The heartbeat timer, refreshed by the host’s periodic heartbeat, catches a host that has gone silent, which is what a kernel panic produces. The chunk lease, refreshed only by a valid admitted proposal, catches a host that is still alive and still sending heartbeats but no longer delivering valid motion. When either expires, the MCU intervenes without waiting for host acknowledgment, selecting the precompiled, state-matched stop suffix within its validated entry envelope and actively commanding motor torques to bring the robot to a controlled halt.

Fallacy: A mathematically correct safety filter guarantees safety even when underlying state feedback has drifted.

A barrier certificate evaluated against a drifted or corrupted state estimate provides zero physical protection. The mobile base’s 40 mm tracking certificate bounds true physical position error only under qualified state feedback assumptions. In practice, comparing the reference trajectory against encoder-reported position serves merely as a runtime proxy; that agreement alone cannot verify the actual physical bound. Wheel slip or an optical encoder with an undetected scale bias can lead the state estimator to report that the base is farther from the aisle rack than its true physical location. The CBF-QP solver then easily satisfies its barrier inequality on the corrupted estimate, while the physical base exhausts clearance margins that the stopping budget never granted. Only an independent sensing channel, such as an IMU or a safety lidar measuring direct range to the rack, can expose this divergence, and only if the tracking error exceeds the diagnostic monitor’s observability threshold. System qualification must bound how far the estimate can drift before detection occurs. The stopping budget must account for this worst-case drift in its localization allowance \(\LocalizationBound\), rather than crediting that same uncertainty to the dynamic tracking inset. To preserve formal safety guarantees, the qualification framework must explicitly document which physical error source each budget allowance covers.

Architect’s countermeasure: Implement redundant, heterogeneous sensor cross-checks that continuously validate kinematic wheel odometry against independent physical channels, such as optical flow, time-of-flight lidar, or secondary motor encoders. Expand the safety filter’s localization allowance \(\LocalizationBound\) to absorb the maximum unobservable drift that can accumulate before diagnostic cross-checks trip, guaranteeing that the contracted safe set remains physically invariant even under worst-case sensor degradation.

Pitfall: Coupling safety braking authority to stochastic semantic classification rather than deterministic spatial clearance.

Emergency stopping authority must be bound to deterministic spatial clearance rather than upstream semantic classification. When an automated vehicle conditions collision avoidance on object classification and trajectory forecasting, perception errors can silently disable emergency intervention. The March 2018 Tempe collision, analyzed in When the Responsibilities Fail Together, shows the sequence (figure 11). Although the developmental automated driving system (ADS) first detected the pedestrian pushing a bicycle 5.6 s before collision, repeated perception toggling between vehicle, other, and bicyclist classifications reset the tracking filter, discarding historical motion at each switch and treating the obstacle as an unpredicted static entity (National Transportation Safety Board 2019, sec. 1.5.5.2). When the pipeline finally registered an imminent crash 1.2 s before impact, high-level policy suppressed actuator intervention for a full 1-second delay to avoid vehicle swerving, after which the planner issued only a gradual deceleration request. The architectural failure sits at the base of the timeline. The vehicle’s factory-installed Volvo autonomous emergency braking (AEB) interlock had been suppressed during ADS operation. An independent geometric safety barrier avoids these cascading failures by separating spatial clearance from semantic inference. It commands an emergency stop based solely on physical distance and closing speed, regardless of what label the neural perception pipeline assigns to the obstacle.

Architect’s countermeasure: Enforce the causal separation of perception and safety by establishing an independent, non-semantic geometric barrier. A safety-rated sensor channel, such as a time-of-flight lidar curtain, radar, or safety scanner, must maintain direct hardware veto authority to command a Category 1 stop. This safety layer evaluates occupancy and closing rate directly, completely bypassing the neural perception pipeline, semantic classifiers, and trajectory forecasting models.

Fallacy: Assuming a continuous-time barrier certificate holds under discrete Zero-Order Hold sampling without an inter-sample kinematic buffer.

Checking a continuous-time barrier inequality at discrete sampling instants does not guarantee inter-sample safety. In digital control architectures, an actuator applies a constant torque or current command over the entire zero-order hold (ZOH) interval \(\PermissionLoopPeriod =\) 1 ms. If an active-set QP solver computes a minimally invasive control input at sample \(\TimeInstant_k\) that places the plant directly on the boundary of the safe set (\(\SafetyBoundaryManifold\)), continuous physical dynamics unfold during the intervening hold period. Between \(\TimeInstant_k\) and \(\TimeInstant_{k+1}\), physical acceleration under the held actuator command can drive the true state across the boundary before the next control cycle executes. Equating a continuous barrier certificate \(\BarrierFunctionRate + \LinearBarrierDecayRate \BarrierFunction \ge 0\) directly with discrete-time invariance without an inter-sample buffer invites boundary violations whose severity grows with velocity and sample period.

Architect’s countermeasure: Contract discrete-time barrier certificates by the worst-case inter-sample kinematic drift buffer, \(\FiniteChange \ZohHoldTravel = \BodySpeed \PermissionLoopPeriod + \frac{1}{2} \ProfileAccelerationBound \PermissionLoopPeriod^2\) evaluated at the highest reachable speed \(\BodySpeed\). In a 1 kHz control loop operating at the 1.5 m/s drive limit with an independently stipulated approach-acceleration upper bound of 2 m/s², this inter-sample displacement bound equals 1.501 mm, the drift that the discretization condition of section 12.6.3 charges. Enforcing the barrier condition against the tightened safe set \(\TightenedDiscreteSafeSet = \{\MechanicalPlantState \mid \BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState) \ge \FiniteChange \ZohHoldTravel\}\) preserves physical clearance throughout the hold duration when the barrier measures spatial distance. When accounting for this margin in the stopping budget, architects must avoid double-counting, because any travel already allocated to the budget’s one-tick fault detection interval already covers this displacement. Furthermore, when evaluating non-spatial or kinetic barriers, engineers cannot rely solely on a geometric displacement bound; the implementation must bound the worst-case evolution of the barrier function itself under the held input across the full sample interval \([\TimeInstant_k, \TimeInstant_{k+1}]\).

12.12 Summary

Runtime enforcement grants an admitted plan continuing, conditional permission to drive physical hardware. On every control cycle the enforcer evaluates live plant state against the tracking bound, the stopping reservation, and the actuator authority that remains. It passes a safe proposal through, projects a correctable one back into the admissible set, and selects a resident fallback, without waiting for the learned proposer, when no correction exists or the proposer falls silent.

Clearance is a composite margin, not a distance read from a map. At the warehouse mobile manipulator’s 1.3 m/s aisle speed, travel during the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) takes 173.68 mm, the \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) stop suffix 605.31 mm, localization and protective margin 150 mm, and the tracking inset 40 mm, for 968.99 mm of the 1.10 m available at the rack end. The remaining 131.01 mm pays for dispatch-to-admission travel (\(\SpeedAtDetection\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 82.3 mm) and leaves a 48.7 mm reserve, so the aisle speed remains a candidate until loaded timing measurements confirm that allocation. A budget built on instantaneous, constant-deceleration braking would have admitted the drive limit, where the jerk-limited stop no longer fits. The budget also holds only while the actuators deliver their assumed deceleration; gravity on a descending payload, bus sag, current ceilings, and thermal throttling all shrink braking authority, so the enforcer must contract the speed envelope before that authority is spent rather than after.

The kinetic barrier turns that reservation into a constraint on the next command. Raw clearance has relative degree two with respect to torque, so a barrier on distance alone leaves the motor no authority at the boundary. Folding approach speed and net braking deceleration into equation 5 lowers the relative degree to one, and its zero level set is the single-joint stopping budget written in state space. Under the qualified delivered-effort error and response-delay contract, the least-change quadratic program removes only the part of a proposal that violates the tightened half-space, which preserves useful task progress when the corrected motion remains task-admissible. An independent per-axis clamp can leave the coupled constraint broken. When contact is the task, force replaces clearance as the margin. Force keeps building through the response interval, so the latch and handover ceilings are approach speeds derived from the force at brake onset and from the effective mass a free or a clamped hand meets, each valid only under its stiffness, deceleration, and geometry premises.

When a lease lapses or a checked premise fails, the arbiter selects directly among PROJECT, HOLD, STOP, and INHIBIT by the plant’s state and the hardware that survives; the rungs are alternatives, not an escalation. Three timer duties detect different failures: the 60 ms chunk lease bounds a stale proposal, the 30 ms host-heartbeat timeout catches a host stall, and the 5 ms MCU self-watchdog catches a firmware deadlock. Beneath these responses sit the certified functions of the drives, the safety scanner, and the arm’s safety controller, which carry the formal protective claim. On the permission path itself, only the safety MCU’s watchdog that revokes authority when the chunk lease expires or the host heartbeat falls silent is a rated safety function. The admission checks are an unrated performance layer whose job is to reduce unnecessary trips of the assumed qualified protective floor (section 12.8.3). The enforcement record ties each check to its detector, its fallback, and the evidence category of every number it uses, so that what the permission path verified stays distinct from what it assumed. The checks and ceilings the permission path applies reduce to the relations in table 7.

Key Takeaways: Conditional enforcement and physical stopping
  • Tracking is a bounded premise: Steady-state tracking guarantees do not bound transient overshoot. Clearance insets must incorporate worst-case transient tracking errors validated experimentally across the robot’s full operating envelope.
  • The stopping budget dictates allowable speed: Sensing latency, reaction delays, \(\DifferentiabilityClass^2\) braking trajectories, tracking error insets, and localization margins all compound into a single admission check. True physical clearance, rather than motor drive limits, determines how fast the machine may safely move.
  • Control barriers must account for kinetic energy: Because spatial distance has relative degree two with respect to joint torque, a position-only barrier cannot guarantee immediate forward invariance. Formulating a kinetic barrier that incorporates velocity yields an affine torque half-space for safe single-step execution.
  • In physical contact, force replaces distance as the margin: Mechanical force continues to build throughout the latency of a braking response. Latching stops must be dimensioned from the projected peak force at brake onset, and approach velocities during human handover must be capped based on effective inertia and structural compliance.
  • Distinct timer duties expose different failure modes: Trajectory chunk leases, host-to-controller heartbeats, microcontroller watchdog cycles, and sensor timestamps expose different failure modes. No single software timeout can protect against all failure modes.
  • Fallback execution is state- and hardware-dependent: Safely executing a least-change projection, powered zero-velocity hold, controlled deceleration ramp, or torque inhibit requires specific surviving hardware and dynamic state. The fallback must be selected while the admissible control set is still nonempty; an empty chosen intersection loses that filter’s claim, while an alternate response needs its own qualified claim.
  • The software permission path complements certified hardware: Safety-rated laser scanners, certified drives, and emergency stop interlocks provide the formal protective baseline. The running architecture assumes a rated lease-expiry and heartbeat watchdog on the permission path. The algorithmic admission checks are unrated and operate above this floor to keep operation smooth without tripping certified hard stops.
  • Every intervention requires an audit record: The enforcer must bind every command token to verified sensor inputs and log all constraint modifications or fallback triggers to an audit log. This logging ensures system designers can audit safety margins on physical hardware and validate assumptions before production deployment.

This chapter is where the third law, proposal is not permission, stops being a design stance and becomes a mechanism. A learned proposal reaches the drives only as a candidate. It earns authority one tick at a time, and only while its evidence is fresh, its lease unexpired, its tracking error within bound, and the stop it would need still inside the clearance. Nothing the proposer emits can extend that authority, and the response that replaces it never waits for the proposer.

A complete runtime enforcement architecture joins the flexibility of learned policies to the determinism of physical safety guarantees. It reserves stopping clearance dynamically, projects candidate controls when a feasible correction exists, and selects a state-matched fallback when communication fails or an invariant breaks. Mathematical safety certificates and algorithmic barrier functions, however, are only as dependable as the underlying real-time compute fabric. Interference through whatever the permission path still shares with the proposers, a board link, a supply rail, a clock, or in some placements a die and a memory bus, can silently consume time that the physical budget already assigned to braking. The final engineering obligation is therefore empirical: verifying that the entire permission pipeline strictly meets its real-time deadlines under worst-case system load on the physical hardware.

12.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The safety certificates developed in this chapter trace their intellectual lineage to classical questions about boundary invariance that predate digital computing. In 1892, Aleksandr Lyapunov demonstrated that one can prove the asymptotic stability of a dynamical system by constructing a scalar, energy-like function, bypassing the intractable problem of solving nonlinear differential equations directly. Mitio Nagumo extended this geometric intuition from isolated equilibrium points to entire sets (Nagumo 1942): a closed set traps all future trajectories if and only if the vector field at every point along its boundary points inward or tangent to the boundary surface. Decades later, Aubin formalized this boundary principle for controlled systems within viability theory (Aubin 1991), characterizing the viability kernel as the set of states from which at least one admissible control input can prevent constraint violations indefinitely. The differential games of Isaacs (1965) introduced an adversarial perspective against worst-case disturbances, which Mitchell, Bayen, and Tomlin later computed on numerical grids through Hamilton-Jacobi reachability analysis (Mitchell et al. 2005). Prajna and Jadbabaie translated these boundary arguments into barrier certificates for algebraic system verification (Prajna and Jadbabaie 2004), and Wieland and Allgöwer framed the barrier condition as an affine inequality directly over the control input (Wieland and Allgöwer 2007). Ames and collaborators then combined this affine formulation with real-time optimization, creating the control barrier function quadratic program (CBF-QP) whose solve time can lie in the microsecond range for selected problem sizes and platforms (Ames et al. 2014; Ames et al. 2019). Contemporary systems engineering treats control barrier functions, reachability analysis, and predictive backup sets as a unified family of safety filters, each continuously monitoring an unverified control proposal against a verified fallback action that remains physically executable (Hsu et al. 2024).

Mitchell, Ian M., Alexandre M. Bayen, and Claire J. Tomlin. 2005. “A Time-Dependent Hamilton-Jacobi Formulation of Reachable Sets for Continuous Dynamic Games.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 50 (7): 947–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.2005.851439.
Ames, Aaron D, Jessy W Grizzle, and Paulo Tabuada. 2014. “Control Barrier Function Based Quadratic Programs with Application to Adaptive Cruise Control.” 53rd IEEE Conference on Decision and Control, 6271–78.
Hogan, Neville. 1985. “Impedance Control: An Approach to Manipulation: Part i–Theory, Part II–Implementation, Part III–Applications.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 107 (1): 1–24.
Ferraguti, Federica, Cristian Secchi, and Cesare Fantuzzi. 2013. “A Tank-Based Approach to Impedance Control with Variable Stiffness.” 2013 IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 4948–53. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2013.6631284.

The structural pattern that wraps an analytical certificate around a complex model originates in runtime assurance for aerospace and industrial automation. To deploy advanced, unverified control software without risking catastrophic failure, Sha and colleagues designed the Simplex architecture (Seto et al. 1998; Sha 2001). Simplex allowed a high-performance experimental controller to drive a live plant while an analytically verified baseline controller and a deterministic switching monitor stood ready to reclaim authority whenever the state neared the edge of the recoverable envelope. Alshiekh and collaborators later transplanted this runtime assurance principle into reinforcement learning under the rubric of shielding (Alshiekh et al. 2018), using reactive temporal logic specifications to intercept and correct unsafe exploratory actions before they reached the actuators. Physical embodiment, however, introduces contact dynamics that purely kinematic boundary checks cannot govern. Hogan transformed robotic manipulation by introducing impedance control (Hogan 1985), which regulates the dynamic relationship (stiffness, damping, and inertia) between environmental forces and manipulator motion instead of tracking brittle position trajectories. Willems provided the rigorous foundation for analyzing such interactions through dissipativity theory (Willems 1972), which formalizes the energy bookkeeping required to guarantee stability across interconnected subsystems. Ferraguti and co-authors embedded that bookkeeping directly into digital software via virtual energy tanks (Ferraguti et al. 2013). By tracking energy dissipation within the controller’s internal state, energy tanks restrict command authority when stored reserves deplete, preventing explosive instability during abrupt contact or communication latency.

The requirement that a fail-safe mechanism must operate independently of the software it monitors emerged from painful engineering lessons. The Therac-25 accidents recounted earlier in this chapter illustrated the fatal cost of replacing electromechanical safety interlocks with software routines (Leveson and Turner 1993); race conditions in unverified code bypassed intended protections and delivered lethal radiation doses to patients. International functional safety standards codified this experience by mandating strict architectural isolation between rated safety functions and the operational systems they supervise (International Electrotechnical Commission 2010; International Organization for Standardization 2023a). In robotic drivetrains, this separation extends down to hardware-level stop functions, such as safe torque off and safe operating stop, implemented directly within power electronics and drive logic (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016b). When runtime enforcement relies on software, temporal predictability becomes just as vital as functional correctness. Worst-case execution-time (WCET) analysis, surveyed comprehensively by Wilhelm and colleagues (Wilhelm et al. 2008), combines execution models, platform assumptions, and timing evidence to justify latency bounds; empirical observations alone do not establish a certified worst case. The architecture presented in this chapter unites these distinct traditions. A learned policy proposes high-performance actions, an online filter derived from physical stopping budgets preserves forward invariance under its qualified assumptions, and an isolated safety floor supplies its separately qualified protective response. A tamper-evident enforcement log records the exact state evidence that justified each intervention. For further reading, six works trace these ideas from their foundations to current practice:

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2010. IEC 61508:2010: Functional Safety of Electrical/Electronic/Programmable Electronic Safety-Related Systems. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Organization for Standardization. 2023a. ISO 13849-1:2023: Safety of Machinery, Safety-Related Parts of Control Systems, Part 1: General Principles for Design. International Organization for Standardization.
International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016b. IEC 61800-5-2:2016: Adjustable Speed Electrical Power Drive Systems, Part 5-2: Safety Requirements, Functional. International Electrotechnical Commission.
Wilhelm, Reinhard, Jakob Engblom, Andreas Ermedahl, Niklas Holsti, Stephan Thesing, David Whalley, Guillem Bernat, et al. 2008. “The Worst-Case Execution-Time Problem: Overview of Methods and Survey of Tools.” ACM Transactions on Embedded Computing Systems 7 (3): 36:1–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/1347375.1347389.
  • Blanchini (1999) surveys set invariance in control, tracing the theory from Nagumo’s boundary conditions to algorithmic methods for computing polyhedral and ellipsoidal invariant sets, providing the clearest geometric map of the state space that barrier certificates supervise.
  • Aubin (1991) develops viability theory, the mathematical framework governing the states from which a constrained system can remain safe indefinitely, and the formal theoretical foundation for the kinetic barriers and viability kernels used throughout this chapter.
  • Sha (2001) presents the foundational runtime-assurance argument: a complex, unverified controller may retain command authority only while an analytically verified baseline controller, operating within a known recoverable region, stands ready to safely intervene.
  • Leveson and Turner (1993) reconstructs the Therac-25 radiation therapy accidents and remains the definitive case study demonstrating why safety interlocks must never depend solely on the software systems they are designed to protect.
  • Ames et al. (2019) provides a comprehensive tutorial on control barrier functions and quadratic programming safety filters, covering both mathematical underpinnings and physical robotics deployments, and serving as the natural follow-up to section 12.6.
  • Hsu et al. (2024) unifies control barrier functions, Hamilton-Jacobi reachability, and predictive backup sets into an overarching safety-filter framework, analyzing how learned neural policies can be trained alongside runtime filters to avoid boundary chattering, directly addressing the co-design challenges raised in section 12.7.1.
Blanchini, Franco. 1999. “Set Invariance in Control.” Automatica 35 (11): 1747–67.
Aubin, Jean-Pierre. 1991. Viability Theory. Systems & Control: Foundations & Applications. Birkhäuser.
Sha, Lui. 2001. “Using Simplicity to Control Complexity.” IEEE Software 18 (4): 20–28.
Leveson, Nancy G., and Clark S. Turner. 1993. “An Investigation of the Therac-25 Accidents.” Computer 26 (7): 18–41.
Ames, Aaron D., Samuel Coogan, Magnus Egerstedt, Gennaro Notomista, Koushil Sreenath, and Paulo Tabuada. 2019. “Control Barrier Functions: Theory and Applications.” 2019 18th European Control Conference (ECC), 3420–31. https://doi.org/10.23919/ECC.2019.8796030.
Hsu, Kai-Chieh, Haimin Hu, and Jaime F. Fisac. 2024. “The Safety Filter: A Unified View of Safety-Critical Control in Autonomous Systems.” Annual Review of Control, Robotics, and Autonomous Systems 7: 47–72. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-control-071723-102940.

What’s Next: From barrier certificates to SoC placement
Can the learned proposers share a chassis, and in some placements a die and a memory bus, with the permission path without delaying it? The enforcement record declares a worst-case cycle inside the permission deadline on every tick. That deadline is a claim about silicon, and a barrier result holds only while the permission path meets it. Every budget in this chapter has assumed the placement Part I adopted as a hypothesis, a discrete safety microcontroller physically separate from the proposers’ processor. Workload Placement puts that hypothesis under load. It partitions the learned proposers and the permission path across compute fabrics and names the loaded tests that would show whether memory starvation, clock throttling, or bus contention can delay permission or fallback, recording them in the placement record.
